Ultracategories via Kan extensions of relative monads Thanks: 2020 MSC : Primary: 18N15; secondary: 18C15, 18C20, 03G30, 03C20. The research is supported by the Agence Nationale de la Recherche (ANR), project ANR-23-CE48-0012-01.
Umberto Tarantino, Joshua Wrigley

Abstract
. Many structured categories of interest are most naturally described as algebras for a relative monad, but turn out nonetheless to be algebras for an ordinary monad. We show that, under suitable hypotheses, the left oplax Kan extension of a relative 2-monad on categories yields a pseudomonad having the same category of colax algebras. In particular, we apply this to the study of ultracategories to recover the ‘ultracompletion’ pseudomonad.



Introduction

Monad theory allows the category theorist to recognise algebraic structure in surprising places, such as compact Hausdorff spaces, which are famously the algebras for the
ultrafilter monad
on sets
[
KG71
,
Man76
,
Lei13
]
. The ultrafilter monad is an example of a
codensity monad
, whose underlying functor is the right Kan extension of the inclusion of finite sets into sets along itself. Our aim in this paper is to make
ultracategories
emerge just as naturally, as algebras for a universally induced monad on categories.

Ultracategories offer a solution to a recurring problem in categorical logic: on its own, the category of models and model homomorphisms of a first-order theory does not determine the theory up to any useful notion of logical equivalence (see e.g.
[
Las82
]
and
[
Joh02
, Remark D3.5.4]
). To reconstruct the theory we must endow the category of models with additional abstract structure, such as an
ultrastructure
. Ultrastructure was introduced by Makkai
[
Mak87
]
with the aim of capturing the formal aspects of forming
ultraproducts
of models. The canonical ultrastructure on a category of models, given by the familiar notion of an ultraproduct
[
CK73
, §4]
, provides the necessary extra structure to recover the theory up to
Morita equivalence
(see
[
Mak87
, Theorem 4.1]
and
[
Mak88
, Theorem 3.2]
). In this sense, ultrastructure plays the role of the usual Stone topology on the set of models of a propositional theory – allowing for a Stone-like duality for first-order logic – and thus behaves as a kind of topological gadget on a category. More specifically, ultracategories can be seen as a categorification of compact Hausdorff spaces: indeed, the datum of an ultrastructure on a set
X
coincides with that of a compact Hausdorff topology on
X

[
Lur18
, Theorem 3.1.5]
.

At its core, an ultrastructure on a category
C
consists of, for each
X
-indexed sequence of objects
h\colon X\to C
and each ultrafilter
\nu
on
X
, a choice of an object
h^{C}(\nu)\in C
intended to play the role of the ultraproduct. This datum is called a
pre-ultracategory
in
[
Mak87
]
, and its ‘algebraic’ flavour was already recognised in
[
Mar95
]
. Therein, Marmolejo shows that a class of ultracategories appears by first considering a 2-monad on
{\bf CAT}
, the 2-category of categories, and then taking a second 2-monad on the category of algebras. The question of whether Makkai’s ultracategories can be characterised as algebras for a monad on
{\bf CAT}
is however still open. Later, Lurie
[
Lur18
]
introduced a new axiomatisation of ultracategories and their morphisms which captures sufficient data so as to recover, and improve, Makkai’s reconstruction result
[
Lur18
, Theorems 2.2.2 and 2.3.1]
. Lurie’s ultracategories were proved in
[
Ham25
]
to be colax algebras for a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
, which had been dubbed the
ultracompletion
pseudomonad in
[
Ros24
]
. As much as Lurie’s axiomatisation of an ultrastructure is significantly simpler than Makkai’s, as noted in
[
Di 22
]
, the origin of the axioms still seems obscure.

The main novelty of our approach is to embed the theory of ultracategories within the field of
relative monad theory
. Starting from the ‘no-iteration’ presentation of monads described in
[
Man76
,
MW10
]
, relative monads were introduced in
[
ACU10
,
ACU15
]
as a generalisation of monads where the underlying functor need not be an endofunctor. In essence, just as a monad
T\colon C\to C
can be specified by choosing a lift
Tc^{\prime}\to Tc
for each arrow
c^{\prime}\to Tc
in
C
, a relative monad
T\colon D\to C
over a fixed
root
functor
J\colon D\to C
is given by choosing a lift
Td\to Tc
for each arrow
Jd\to Tc
in
C
. Relative monads – and their 2-dimensional analogues, the
relative pseudomonads
of
[
FGHW18
]
– turn out to be a flexible notion, incorporating many examples of functors which are ‘almost’ a monad but cannot be iterated: most notably, the presheaf construction on small categories (see e.g.
[
ASS25
]
). The key observation making this paper possible is that the datum of a pre-ultracategory resembles that of an algebra for a relative monad over the inclusion
{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
of sets as discrete categories. Specifically, the functor underlying the monad is the composite of the ultrafilter monad
\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf Set}
with the inclusion
{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
itself.

Since their introduction, relative monads were linked with the theory of Kan extensions since we often want to extend, universally, a relative monad to an ordinary monad. Indeed, in
[
ACU15
]
the authors show that, for a suitable “well-behaved” root
J\colon D\to C
, the algebras for a
J
-relative monad
T\colon D\to C
can be realised as algebras for a monad on
C
whose underlying functor is the left Kan extension of
T
along
J
. A naïve 2-categorical generalisation of the same “well-behavedness” assumptions would not be satisfied by the inclusion
{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
involved in our motivating example. In particular, one of these assumptions is that the root functor
J\colon D\to C
is
dense
, i.e. every object of
C
is a colimit of objects in the image of
J
. This would not be satisfied by the inclusion
{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
, since a colimit of discrete categories is itself discrete.

Our intention in this paper is to take the first step towards a more general framework to ‘unrelativise’ relative 2-monads in a similar fashion to
[
ACU15
]
, with the aim to realise (a notion of) ultracategories as algebras for a monad on
{\bf CAT}
. As in
[
Ros24
,
Ham25
]
, our strategy to achieve this will be to construct, starting from a category
C
, a category
\widetilde{\beta}C
of
formal ultraproducts
in
C
given by triples of a set
X
, an
X
-indexed sequence of objects
h\colon X\to C
, and an ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
. The intuition here is that
C
is an ultracategory if such formal ultraproducts can be ‘realised’ in
C
by means of a functor
\widetilde{\beta}C\to C
. In particular, if
C
is the category of models of a first-order theory
\mathbb{T}
, a formal ultraproduct in
C
corresponds to a family
\set{M_{x}}_{x\in X}
of models of
\mathbb{T}
together with an ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
, which can be mapped to the ‘actual’ ultraproduct
\prod_{\nu}M_{\bullet}
(see Remark
2.3
). Crucially, our construction of
\widetilde{\beta}C
does not depend on any specific property of ultrafilters or ultraproducts, allowing us to generalise to other contexts.

Our contribution

We single out a notion of ultracategories emerging naturally as algebras for a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
universally induced by the ultrafilter monad. Starting from the observation that
\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf Set}
lifts to a relative 2-monad
\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf CAT}
over the inclusion
{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
, we will define
weak ultracategories
as colax algebras for this relative 2-monad. Concretely, weak ultracategories are defined by the same data as Lurie’s ultracategories, but with a slightly weaker axiomatisation. Their morphisms, however, coincide precisely with Lurie’s
ultrafunctors

[
Lur18
, Definition 1.4.1]
. Thus, weak ultracategories can still serve as a framework for reconstruction theorems in logic (
[
Mak87
, Theorem 4.1 and Corollary 8.3]
,
[
Lur18
, Theorem 2.2.2]
).

To arrive at the (pseudo)monadicity of weak ultracategories over
{\bf CAT}
, we will extend the results of
[
ACU15
, §3, 4]
by introducing
left oplax Kan extensions
(Definition
3.3
), analogues in the lax setting of (pointwise) left Kan extensions, and we will give an explicit construction of left oplax Kan extensions for
{\bf CAT}
-valued 2-functors. The main results of the paper can be summed up in the following theorem.

Theorem

(
4.13
and
5.4
)
.

Let
T\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
be a relative 2-monad over a 2-functor
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
and let
\tilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
denote the left oplax Kan extension of
T
along
J
. Under the assumptions that:

(1)

\mathcal{B}
has a terminal object
1_{\mathcal{B}}
that is preserved by
J
,

(2)

\mathcal{B}
admits all oplax colimits for diagrams of shape
{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}
for
b\in\mathcal{B}
and they are preserved by
J
,

(3)

and
J
is 2-fully faithful,

\tilde{T}
carries the structure of a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
whose 2-category of colax algebras is isomorphic to that of
T
.

Pseudomonadicity of weak ultracategories will then follow by applying the previous theorem to the
relative ultrafilter 2-monad

\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf CAT}
. Consequently, we conclude that the
weak ultracompletion
pseudomonad
\widetilde{\beta}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
is obtained via a combination of Kan extensions and oplax Kan extensions (Corollary
6.1
). Thus, if “ultraproducts are categorically inevitable”
[
Lei13
]
, so too are weak ultracategories.

Overview

The paper proceeds as follows.

(1)

First, we recall some preliminaries on relative 2-monads and their 2-categories of colax algebras.

(2)

Second, we describe our motivating example of colax algebras for a relative 2-monad: weak ultracategories. We also deduce that, in the relative monadic approach, it automatically follows that
ultrasets
, i.e. sets endowed with (weak) ultrastructure, are precisely the compact Hausdorff spaces (Remark
2.7
, cf.
[
Lur18
, Theorem 3.1.5]
).

(3)

We will ‘unrelativise’ a relative 2-monad on
{\bf CAT}
by taking its
left oplax Kan extension
, which is described in Section
3
.

(4)

Next, in Section
4
, under appropriate hypotheses on the root 2-functor, we endow the left oplax Kan extension of a relative 2-monad on
{\bf CAT}
with the structure of a pseudomonad. In order to do this, we will first introduce a ‘relative composition’ operation on the category of
{\bf CAT}
-valued 2-functors and oplax transformations, making it into a
skew-monoidal category
.

(5)

In Section
5
we show that colax algebras of a relative 2-monad on
{\bf CAT}
coincide with those of its left oplax Kan extension, seen as a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
as in Section
4
.

(6)

Finally, we combine our earlier results to deduce that weak ultracategories are pseudomonadic over
{\bf CAT}
, where the underlying 2-functor of the pseudomonad is obtained by taking Kan extensions and oplax Kan extensions. As another application of our framework, we introduce
prime categories
as an ‘ordered’ analogue of weak ultracategories induced by the
prime upper filter monad
on the category of posets.

1.
Relative monads and their algebras

We begin by reviewing the relevant notions from the theory of relative monads and their algebras, both for the convenience of the reader and so as to fix notations.

1.1.
2-categories

We will assume basic knowledge of 2-category theory. By
2-category
,
2-functor
,
2-monad
etc., we mean the strictest 2-categorical notion, e.g. a 2-functor preserves identity and composition on the nose. In particular, by a
2-fully faithful
2-functor
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
we mean a 2-functor inducing isomorphisms
{\mathcal{B}}({b},{b^{\prime}})\cong{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{Jb^{\prime}})
between hom-categories, rather than equivalences. We write
{\bf CAT}
for the 2-category of (large) categories, with
[C,D]
denoting the category of functors
C\to D
. We refer the reader to, e.g.,
[
JY20
]
for more details. For a category
C
, we will systematically abuse notation by writing
c\in C
and
f\colon c\to c^{\prime}\in C
in place of
c\in\operatorname{Ob}(C)
and
f\colon c\to c^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Arr}(C)
respectively.

1.2.
Relative 2-monads

Recall from
[
Man76
,
MW10
]
that a monad on a category
C
can be equivalently described as an
extension system

\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
, that is:

(1)

for each
c\in C
, an object
Tc\in C
and a
unit arrow

\eta_{c}\colon c\to Tc\in C
,

(2)

for each pair
d,c\in C
, an
extension function

(-)^{\ast}\colon{C}({d},{Tc})\to{C}({Td},{Tc})
,

satisfying equational axioms (see Definition
1.1
below). In particular, we recover the usual description in terms of an endofunctor
T\colon C\to C
by letting
Tf\coloneqq(\eta_{c^{\prime}}\circ f)^{*}
for an arrow
f\colon c\to c^{\prime}\in C
.

In
[
ACU15
]
, this definition is extended to that of a
relative monad
, which allows for an underlying functor between different categories. The
relative pseudomonads
of
[
FGHW18
]
are then a further 2-dimensional generalisation, which we here recall in the particular case which will be used in this paper: namely, that of a
relative 2-monad
in the sense of
[
AM24
]
– i.e. where the root pseudofunctor is a 2-functor and where all structure isomorphisms are taken to be the identity.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a 2-functor between 2-categories, which we will refer to as the
root
. A
J
-relative 2-monad

\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
is the datum of:

(1)

for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
, an object
Tb\in\mathcal{A}
and a
unit 1-cell

\eta_{b}\colon Jb\to Tb\in\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

for each pair
a,b\in\mathcal{B}
, an
extension functor

(-)^{\ast}\colon{\mathcal{A}}({Ja},{Tb})\to{\mathcal{A}}({Ta},{Tb})
,

satisfying:

(a)

\eta_{b}^{*}=\operatorname{id}_{Tb}
for each object
b\in\mathcal{B}
;

(b)

f^{*}\circ\eta_{c}=f
for each 1-cell
f\colon Jc\to Tb\in\mathcal{A}
;

(c)

(f^{*}\circ g)^{*}=f^{*}\circ g^{*}
for each pair of 1-cells
f\colon Jc\to Tb,g\colon Jd\to Tc\in\mathcal{A}
.

Under this definition,
T
can be extended to a 2-functor
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
, where the action on an arrow
f\colon b\to b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
is given by
(\eta_{b^{\prime}}\circ Jf)^{*}\colon Tb\to Tb^{\prime}
, while
\eta
and
(-)^{*}
become 2-natural transformations
J\Rightarrow T
and
{\mathcal{A}}({J-},{T-})\Rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}({T-},{T-})
respectively (cf.
[
FGHW18
, Proposition 4.7]
).

Example 1.2
.

Relative 2-monads over the identity 2-functor
\operatorname{id}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
coincide with 2-monads on
\mathcal{A}
in the usual sense (cf.
[
ASS25
, Remarks 3.4, 3.5]
).

Example 1.3
.

For a 2-monad
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
on
\mathcal{B}
and a 2-fully faithful 2-functor
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
, the 2-functor
JT\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
carries the structure of a
J
-relative 2-monad where:

(1)

for
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the unit is given by
J\eta_{b}\colon Jb\to JTb
;

(2)

for
b,c\in\mathcal{B}
, the extension functor is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{A}}({Jc},{JTb})}\cong{{\mathcal{B}}({c},{Tb})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{B}}({Tc},{Tb})}\cong{{\mathcal{B}}({JTc},{JTb})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{*}}

which we still denote as
(-)^{*}
, so that for a 1-cell
f\colon c\to Tb
in
\mathcal{B}
we can unambiguously write
Jf^{*}
for
(Jf)^{*}=J(f^{*})
.

We omit the routine verification that these data satisfy Definition
1.1
.

1.3.
Algebras for a relative 2-monad

Relative 2-monads admit notions of algebras which extend the usual notion of algebra for a (2-)monad. The 2-dimensional setting, in particular, allows us to distinguish between
lax
,
colax
,
pseudo
and
strict
algebras depending on the 2-cells involved in the definitions, and similarly for their morphisms. We recall here the definitions that we will use in this paper, namely those regarding
colax algebras
; for a more extensive treatment, we refer the reader to
[
ASS25
]
.

The construction of the 2-category of colax algebras given in Definition
1.4
below is not concocted in a vacuum. It is intended to capture a 2-dimensional generalisation of the universal property of the category of algebras for a relative monad amongst all resolutions
[
ACU15
, Theorem 2.12]
, itself a generalisation of the universal property of the category of Eilenberg-Moore algebras for a monad (see
[
EM65
, Theorem 2.2]
). However, the necessary literature regarding ‘colax-resolutions’ of relative 2-monads is not yet extant, the closest parallel being given for pseudo-resolutions in
[
ASS25
, §6]
. We hope that the example of weak ultracategories (Definition
2.1
) serves as the catalyst for further research generalising
[
ACU15
,
ASS25
]
to the colax setting – see also Example
1.7
and
[
ASS25
, Remark 3.4]
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a 2-functor and let
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
be a
J
-relative 2-monad, which we abbreviate to
T
.

A
colax algebra
for
T
is a tuple
\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}
consisting of:

(1)

an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
, a functor
(-)^{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{A})\to{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{A})
, called the
extension operator
;

(3)

a family of 2-cells
\Gamma_{h}\colon{h}^{A}\circ\eta_{b}\Rightarrow h
for each
h\colon Jb\to A\in\mathcal{A}
, natural in
h
;

(4)

a family of 2-cells
\Delta_{h,k}\colon{h}^{A}\circ k^{\ast}\Rightarrow({h}^{A}\circ k)^{A}
for each
h\colon Jb\to A,k\colon Jc\to Tb\in\mathcal{A}
, natural in
h
and
k
,

satisfying the commutativity equations

(a)\hbox to130.61pt{\vbox to63.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 65.30472pt\lower-30.48172pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

for every
h\colon Jb\to A,k\colon Jc\to Tb,l\colon Jd\to Tc\in\mathcal{A}
. We say that
A
is
strict
if each 2-cell
\Gamma_{h}
and each 2-cell
\Delta_{h,k}
is the identity.

A
colax morphism

\braket{U,\upsilon}\colon{\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}\to\braket{A^{\prime},(-)^{A^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}}}
of colax algebras for
T
consists of:

(1)

a 1-cell
U\colon A\to A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

a family of
2
-cells
\upsilon_{h}\colon U{h}^{A}\Rightarrow{(Uh)}^{A^{\prime}}
for each
h\colon Jb\to A\in\mathcal{A}
, natural in
h
,

satisfying the commutativity equations

for every
h\colon Jb\to A,k\colon Jc\to Tb\in\mathcal{A}
. A
lax morphism
is defined analogously, reversing the direction of each 2-cell
\upsilon_{h}
. In particular, a (co)lax morphism
\braket{U,\upsilon}
is a
pseudomorphism
if each
\upsilon_{h}
is invertible, and it is a
strict morphism
if each
\upsilon_{h}
is the identity.

Let
\braket{U,\upsilon},\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}\colon{\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}\to\braket{A^{\prime},(-)^{A^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}}}
be colax morphisms of colax algebras for
T
. A
transformation

\braket{U,\upsilon}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}
is a 2-cell
\alpha\colon U\Rightarrow U^{\prime}
such that, for each
h\colon Jb\to A\in\mathcal{A}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U{h}^{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}{h}^{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(Uh)}^{A^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(U^{\prime}h)}^{A^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha*{h}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{\prime}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\alpha*h)}^{A^{\prime}}}

commutes. Transformations between lax morphisms are defined analogously.

Proposition 1.5

(
[
ASS25
, §3.9-11]
)
.

Colax algebras, their morphisms and transformations form 2-categories:

(1)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)
, with colax morphisms;

(2)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{lax}^{J}(T)
, with lax morphisms;

(3)

{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{ps}(T)
, with pseudomorphisms;

(4)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{str}^{J}(T)
, with strict morphisms.

Notation 1.6
.

For
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}
, we will write
{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{\star}(T)
to refer to any of the above 2-categories.

Example 1.7
.

Let
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a 2-functor and let
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
be a
J
-relative 2-monad. For each object
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the object
Tb\in\mathcal{A}
admits a (strict) algebra structure determined by the extension functors
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathcal{A}}({Ja},{Tb})\to{\mathcal{A}}({Ta},{Tb})
. The assignment
b\mapsto\braket{Tb,(-)^{\ast},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id}}
extends to a 2-functor
T^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{\star}(T)
which acts as
T
on the underlying objects, 1-cells and 2-cells of
\mathcal{B}
. That is to say, post-composing
T^{\prime}
with the evident forgetful 2-functor
F\colon{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{\star}(T)\to\mathcal{A}
, that sends a colax algebra
\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}
to
A
, returns the 2-functor
T
.

Moreover, the 2-functor
T^{\prime}
yields a
J
-relative colax 2-adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\ .}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

By this we mean that, for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
and each colax algebra
\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}
for
T
, there is a suitably natural adjunction

The counit of the adjunction, at a 1-cell
h\colon Jb\to A
, is given by
\Gamma_{h}\colon h^{A}\circ\eta_{b}\Rightarrow h
, while the unit, at a colax morphism
\braket{U,\upsilon}\colon\braket{Tb,(-)^{\ast},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id}}\to\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}
, is the transformation defined by the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U=U\circ\eta^{*}_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(U\circ\eta_{b})^{A}\ .}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\upsilon_{\eta_{b}}}}

In this sense,
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)
is a
colax-resolution
for the relative 2-monad
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
.

Example 1.8
.

Let
T
be a 2-monad on
\mathcal{B}
and
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
a 2-fully faithful 2-functor, so that
JT
is a
J
-relative 2-monad as in Example
1.3
. Then, by the 2-fully faithfulness of
J
, we have evident 2-fully faithful embeddings
{\rm ColaxAlg}^{\operatorname{id}}_{\star}(T)\hookrightarrow{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{\star}(JT)
whose images are spanned by those algebras having their carrier object in the essential image of
J
.

2.
Weak ultracatagories

Having introduced the colax algebras for a relative 2-monad, we describe our motivating example:
ultracategories
. Let
J\colon{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
denote the the inclusion of sets into categories viewing a set as a discrete category, and let
\braket{\beta,\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
denote the
ultrafilter monad
on
{\bf Set}
, i.e.
\beta
sends a set
X
to the set of ultrafilters on
X
, presented as an extension system. Recall from Example
1.3
that, since
J
is 2-fully faithful, we obtain a
J
-relative 2-monad
\braket{J\beta,J\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
which we dub the
relative ultrafilter 2-monad
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
weak ultracategory
is a colax algebra for the relative ultrafilter 2-monad. For
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}
, we denote with
\mathbf{WUlt}_{\star}
the 2-category
{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{\star}(J\beta)
.

We will now spell out what this means, leaving
J
implicit. First, to provide some intuition, recall by Manes’ theorem
[
Man69
, Proposition 5.5]
that a compact Hausdorff topology on a set
S
can be specified by the structure of a
\beta
-algebra on
S
. Presenting
\beta
as the extension system
\braket{\beta,\eta,(-)^{*}}
, this means that a compact Hausdorff topology on a set
S
can be given by specifying, for each function
h\colon X\to S
, an extension
h^{S}\colon\beta X\to S
. Thinking of
h
as an
X
-indexed sequence of points in
S
,
h^{S}
can then be understood as mapping each ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
to the unique ‘limit’ of the sequence
h
‘with respect’ to
\nu
. The description in terms of a
\beta
-algebra
\beta S\to S
can be recovered by considering the extension of
\operatorname{id}\colon S\to S
.

With these ideas in mind, a weak ultracategory is a category
M
endowed with:

(1)

for each set
X
, a functor
(-)^{M}\colon\left[X,M\right]\to\left[\beta X,M\right]
, which maps an
X
-indexed sequence
h\colon X\to M
of objects in
M
to the functor
h^{M}
sending each ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
to the limit of the sequence
h
with respect to
\nu
;

(2)

a family of natural transformations
\Gamma_{h}\colon h^{M}\circ\eta_{X}\Rightarrow h
for every sequence
h\colon X\to M
of objects in
M
, natural in
h
;

(3)

a family of natural transformations
\Delta_{h,k}\colon h^{M}\circ k^{*}\Rightarrow(h^{M}\circ k)^{M}
for every sequence
h\colon X\to M
of objects in
M
and every sequence
k\colon Y\to\beta X
of ultrafilters on
X
, natural in
h
and
k
,

satisfying the equations

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

for every sequence
h\colon X\to M
of objects in
M
, every sequence
k\colon Y\to\beta X
of ultrafilters on
X
, and every sequence
l\colon Z\to\beta Y
of ultrafilters on
Y
.

Example 2.2
.

The category
{\bf Set}
carries the archetypal structure of an ultracategory. For a function
h\colon X\to{\bf Set}
, i.e. a tuple of sets
\{M_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, the extension
h^{\bf Set}\colon\beta X\to{\bf Set}
acts by sending an ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
to the
ultraproduct

\prod_{\nu}M_{\bullet}\coloneqq\colim_{U\in{\nu}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\prod_{x\in U}M_{x}\right)
.

Remark 2.3
.

In the case of a compact Hausdorff space
S
, the
\beta
-algebra map
\beta S\to S
witnessing the topology can be recovered as the extension of
\operatorname{id}\colon S\to S
. In the case of a weak ultracategory
M
, instead, we cannot obtain an ‘algebra map’ simply by “extending the identity”. Our strategy to turn
M
into an algebra for a (pseudo)monad on
{\bf CAT}
will be to define a category
\widetilde{\beta}M
whose objects are triples
(X\in{\bf Set},h\colon X\to M,\nu\in\beta X)
. The algebra functor
\widetilde{\beta}M\to M
will then map such a triple to the limit
h^{M}(\nu)\in M
of the sequence
h
with respect to
\nu
.

In particular, in the case of a (weak) ultracategory of the form
\operatorname{Mod}({\mathbb{T}})
, the category of models for a first-order theory
\mathbb{T}
, this means that the algebra functor sends a triple
(X\in{\bf Set},h\colon X\to\operatorname{Mod}({\mathbb{T}}),\nu\in\beta X)
, i.e. a sequence of models
\set{M_{x}}_{x\in X}
together with an ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
, to their ultraproduct with respect to
\nu
. In this sense, we can see the category
\widetilde{\beta}(\operatorname{Mod}({\mathbb{T}}))
as the category of
formal ultraproducts
of sequences of models of
\mathbb{T}
(cf.
[
Ros24
]
).

Lurie’s ultracategories can be immediately framed within weak ultracategories. It is evident that – up to currying functors and natural transformations – every ultracategory
à la
Lurie
[
Lur18
, Definition 1.3.1]
is endowed with the same structure as that of a colax algebra for
\braket{J\beta,J\eta,(-)^{*}}
. The only difference is that our axiomatisation is weaker.

Definition 2.4

(
[
Lur18
, Definition 1.3.1]
)
.

A
Lurie-ultracategory
is a weak ultracategory
\braket{M,(-)^{M},\Gamma,\Delta}
such that:

(1)

each natural transformation
\Gamma
is an isomorphism;

(2)

for each sequence
h\colon X\to M
of objects in
M
and each injection
i\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
, the natural transformation
\Delta_{h,\eta_{X}i}
is an isomorphism.

Remark 2.5
.

In detail, our axioms (b) and (c) above precisely correspond to axioms (A) and (C) of
[
Lur18
, Definition 1.3.1]
, while axiom (a) is recovered making use of the two extra assumptions in
[
Lur18
, Corollary 1.3.6]
.

Although our axiomatisation of ultracategories is strictly weaker than Lurie’s (see Example
2.8
), the
left/right ultrafunctors
,
ultrafunctors
, and
transformations
from
[
Lur18
, Definitions 1.4.1-2]
coincide exactly with the
colax/lax morphisms
,
pseudomorphisms
, and
transformations
from Definition
1.4
. In other words, Lurie’s 2-categories
\mathbf{Ult}
and
\mathbf{Ult}^{\text{L}}
2-fully-faithfully embed into
\mathbf{WUlt}_{ps}
and
\mathbf{WUlt}_{co}
respectively. This immediately entails that the logical properties of Lurie’s ultracategories carry over to weak ultracategories, which thus satisfy the desiderata for an axiomatisation of the notion of ultracategories:

(1)

for a small pretopos
P
, its category of models
\operatorname{Mod}({P})\coloneqq{\mathbf{Pretop}}({P},{{\bf Set}})
is a weak ultracategory, and the evaluation functor

\mathsf{ev}\colon P\to{\mathbf{WUlt}_{ps}}({\operatorname{Mod}({P})},{{\bf Set}})\subseteq{\mathbf{WUlt}_{co}}({\operatorname{Mod}({P})},{{\bf Set}})

induces an equivalence between
{\mathbf{WUlt}_{co}}({\operatorname{Mod}({P})},{{\bf Set}})
and the topos
\mathsf{Sh}(P)
as in
[
Lur18
, Theorem 2.2.2]
;

(2)

moreover, the functor
\mathsf{ev}
is an equivalence
P\simeq{\mathbf{WUlt}_{ps}}({\operatorname{Mod}({P})},{{\bf Set}})
itself as in
[
Mak87
, Theorem 4.1]
and
[
Lur18
, Theorem 2.3.1]
, which implies in particular that for each pair of pretoposes
P,P^{\prime}
there is an equivalence of categories

{\mathbf{Pretop}}({P},{P^{\prime}})\simeq{\mathbf{WUlt}_{ps}}({\operatorname{Mod}({P^{\prime}})},{\operatorname{Mod}({P})})

as in
[
Mak87
, Corollary 8.3]
and
[
Lur18
, Corollary 2.3.3]
.

As we will see, the main advantage of our weakening of Lurie’s axioms will be that weak ultracategories coincide with algebras for a universally obtained pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
(Corollary
6.1
; cf. also the discussion in
[
Di 22
]
).

Remark 2.6
.

As already for Lurie’s ultracategories, it is not entirely clear whether Makkai’s ultracategories are weak ultracategories, and moreover if the 2-category
\mathbf{UC}
of Makkai’s ultracategories embeds into
\mathbf{WUlt}_{ps}
. However, denoting with
\mathbf{WUlt}_{0},\mathbf{UC}_{0}
and
\mathbf{Ult}_{0}
respectively the full 2-subcategories of
\mathbf{WUlt}_{ps},\mathbf{UC}
and
\mathbf{Ult}
spanned by those ultracategories of the form
\operatorname{Mod}({P})
for a small pretopos
P
, it follows that

\mathbf{WUlt}_{0}\simeq\mathbf{UC}_{0}\simeq\mathbf{Ult}_{0}

as they are all equivalent to
{\mathbf{Pretop}}^{\operatorname{op}}
via the 2-functor
\operatorname{Mod}({-})
.

Remark 2.7

(Ultrasets)
.

Call a weak ultracategory
\braket{M,(-)^{M},\Gamma,\Delta}
an
ultraset
if the carrier category
M
is actually a set. Then since
{\bf Set}
is discrete on 2-cells, all ultrasets are strict algebras: in particular they are Lurie-ultracategories, and the notion coincides with that of
[
Lur18
, Definition 3.1.1]
. Moreover, all morphisms of ultrasets are strict, and the only transformations between them are identities, so we can unambiguously define the category
\mathbf{UltSet}
of ultrasets. By Example
1.8
, it follows that
\mathbf{UltSet}
is isomorphic to the category of
\beta
-algebras. Applying Manes’ theorem
[
Man69
, Proposition 5.5]
, we deduce that
\mathbf{UltSet}
is isomorphic to the category of compact Hausdorff spaces, thus immediately recovering
[
Lur18
, Theorem 3.1.5]
.

Example 2.8
.

Before moving on, we describe a weak ultracategory that does not satisfy either of the stronger conditions of Definition
2.4
. Consider the
category of elements
for the ultrafilter functor
\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf Set}
, i.e. the category whose objects are pairs
(X,\nu)
consisting of a set
X
and an ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta X
, while an arrow
f\colon(X,\nu)\to(X^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
consists of a function
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
such that
\beta(f)(\nu^{\prime})=\nu
. Note that this coincides with the category
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{E}
of ultrafilters defined in
[
Gar20
, Definition 4]
. We denote this category by
\widetilde{\beta}(1)
: indeed, it follows from the results of Section
6
that
\widetilde{\beta}(1)
is the free weak ultracategory on the trivial category.

Explicitly,
\widetilde{\beta}(1)
is endowed with the following ultrastructure.

(1)

The extension functor is constructed as follows.

Let
h\colon Y\to\widetilde{\beta}(1)
be a function. For each
y\in Y
let
(Ry,\nu_{y})
be the image
h(y)
, and let
\coprod R
denote the coproduct of the family
\set{Ry}{y\in Y}
, with coprojections
c_{y}\colon Ry\hookrightarrow\coprod R
which for simplicity we identify with subset inclusions. Consider then the function:

\textstyle q\colon Y\to\beta\left(\coprod R\right),\qquad y\mapsto\beta(c_{y})(\nu_{y})

which, using the monad structure for
\beta
, lifts to a function
q^{*}\colon\beta Y\to\beta\left(\coprod R\right)
. Explicitly, for an ultrafilter
\nu\in\beta Y
,
q^{*}\nu
is the ultrafilter on
\coprod R
characterised by

S\in q^{*}\nu\iff\set{y\in Y}{S\cap Ry\in\nu_{y}}\in\nu.

Thus, we can define the algebra extension of
h
as the function:

\textstyle h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\colon\beta Y\to\widetilde{\beta}(1),\qquad\nu\mapsto\left(\coprod R,q^{\ast}\nu\right).

Given two functions
h,h^{\prime}\colon Y\to\widetilde{\beta}(1)
, let
\sigma\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
be a natural transformation. For each
y\in Y
, the component
\sigma_{y}\colon(Ry,\nu_{y})\to(R^{\prime}y,\nu^{\prime}_{y})
is in particular a function
\sigma_{y}\colon R^{\prime}y\to Ry
, so there is an induced function
\coprod\sigma\colon\coprod R^{\prime}\to\coprod R
. Thus, we can define the algebra extension
\sigma^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\colon h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\Rightarrow h^{\prime\widetilde{\beta}(1)}
of
\sigma
as the natural transformation whose component at
\nu\in\beta Y
is the arrow

\textstyle\coprod\sigma\colon\left(\coprod R,q^{*}\nu\right)\to\left(\coprod R^{\prime},q^{\prime*}\nu\right)

in
\widetilde{\beta}(1)
.

(2)

For a function
h\colon Y\to\widetilde{\beta}(1)
, the natural transformation
\Gamma_{h}\colon h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\circ\eta_{Y}\Rightarrow h
is defined at
y\in Y
by the arrow

\textstyle c_{y}\colon h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\circ\eta_{Y}(y)=(\coprod R,q^{\ast}\eta_{Y}(y))\to(Ry,\nu_{y})=h(y)

Indeed,
q^{*}\eta_{Y}(y)
is the ultrafilter on
\coprod R
defined by:

S\in q^{*}\eta_{Y}(y)\iff\set{y^{\prime}\in Y}{S\cap Ry^{\prime}\in\nu_{y^{\prime}}}\in\eta_{Y}(y)\iff S\cap Ry\in\nu_{y},

meaning that
q^{*}\eta_{Y}(y)=\beta(c_{y})(\nu_{y})
. Note that this arrow is not invertible, and hence
\Gamma_{h}
is not an isomorphism, whenever there is more than one non-empty
Ry
.

(3)

For a pair of functions
h\colon Y\to\widetilde{\beta}(1)
and
k\colon Z\to\beta Y
, the natural transformation
\Delta_{h,k}\colon h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\circ k^{*}\Rightarrow(h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\circ k)^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}
is defined as follows.

Let
\nu\in\beta Z
. By definition,
h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}(k^{*}\nu)
is the pair
(\coprod R,q^{*}k^{*}\nu)
. On the other hand note that, for each
z\in Z
,
h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}(k(z))=(\coprod R,q^{*}k(z))
, where for
S\subseteq\coprod R
:

S\in q^{*}k(z)\iff\set{y\in Y}{S\cap Ry\in\nu_{y}}\in k(z)

Therefore, by definition:

\textstyle(h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\circ k)^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}(\nu)=(\coprod_{z\in Z}(\coprod R),\bar{q}^{*}\nu)

where, for convenience of notation, we define:

\textstyle\coprod R\coloneqq\set{(y,x)}{y\in Y,x\in Ry}

\textstyle\coprod_{z\in Z}(\coprod R)\coloneqq\set{(z,y,x)}{z\in Z,y\in Y,x\in Ry}

so that, for
T\subseteq\coprod_{z\in Z}(\coprod R)
, we can write:

\displaystyle T\in\bar{q}^{*}\nu

\displaystyle\iff\set{z\in Z}{\set{(y,x)}{y\in Y,x\in Ry:(z,y,x)\in T}\in q^{*}k(z)}\in\nu

With this notation, let
\pi\colon\coprod_{z\in Z}(\coprod R)\to\coprod R
be the obvious projection, and note that
q^{*}k^{*}\nu=\beta(\pi)(\bar{q}^{*}\nu)
. The component of
\Delta_{h,k}
at
\nu\in\beta Z
is given by the arrow

\textstyle\pi\colon h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}(k^{*}\nu)=(\coprod R,q^{*}k^{*}\nu)\to(\coprod_{z\in Z}(\coprod R),\bar{q}^{*}\nu)=(h^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}\circ k)^{\widetilde{\beta}(1)}(\nu).

Note that this arrow need not be invertible, and hence
\Delta_{h,k}
need not be an isomorphism, even if
k=\eta_{Y}\circ i
for some injective function
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
. For a concrete counterexample, take
Y
having at least three distinct elements,
h
such that each
Ry
is finite, and
Z\subseteq Y
consisting of two distinct elements.

3.
Oplax Kan extensions

The 2-endofunctor underlying the ‘unrelativisation’ of a relative 2-monad in
{\bf CAT}
, which we construct in the next section, will be a
left oplax Kan extension
– a generalisation of the notion of a
{\bf CAT}
-enriched left Kan extension
in the sense of
[
Kel05
, §4.20]
. In this section, we describe the universal property defining left oplax Kan extensions and give an explicit construction for
{\bf CAT}
-valued 2-functors.

3.1.
Oplax Kan extensions

We begin by recalling the definition of an oplax transformation between 2-functors.

Definition 3.1

(
[
Str72
,
JY20
, Definitions 1]
)
.

Let
F,G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a pair of 2-functors. An
oplax transformation

\beta\colon F\Rightarrow G
consists of the data of an arrow
\beta_{b}\colon Fb\to Gb
for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
, and a 2-cell
\beta_{f}\colon\beta_{b}\circ F(f)\Rightarrow G(f)\circ\beta_{b^{\prime}}
for each arrow
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b
in
\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, these data must satisfy the coherence and naturality conditions:

(1)

the 2-cells
\beta_{f}
, for
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b\in\mathcal{B}
, define a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(b^{\prime},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}(Fb^{\prime},Gb);}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{b}\ast F(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(-)\ast\beta_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{(-)}}

(2)

\beta_{\operatorname{id}_{b}}
is the identity 2-cell on
\beta_{b}
;

(3)

for each pair
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b,g\colon b^{\prime\prime}\to b^{\prime}
, the pasting on the left coincides with the natural transformation on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Fb^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Fb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{b^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{b^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Fb^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Fb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{b^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{f\circ g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}

A
lax transformation

\beta\colon F\Rightarrow G
is defined in the same way but reversing the direction of each 2-cell
\beta_{f}
. A
strict transformation

\beta\colon F\Rightarrow G
is then one where each
\beta_{f}
is the identity, i.e. simply a 2-natural transformation
F\Rightarrow G
.

We write
{\mathbf{Oplax}}\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}\right]
for the 2-category of 2-functors
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
, oplax transformations, and
modifications
(see e.g.
[
Lei98
]
for the definition); similarly we define
{\mathbf{Lax}}\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}\right]
and
{\mathbf{Str}}\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}\right]
.

Example 3.2
.

Let
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a 2-functor,
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
be a
J
-relative 2-monad, and
\braket{A,(-)^{A},\Gamma,\Delta}
be a colax algebra for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
. Then, the family of extension operators
\set{(-)_{b}^{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{A})\to{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{A})}{b\in\mathcal{B}}
determines a lax transformation

{(-)^{A}\colon{\mathcal{A}}({J-},{A})\Rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}({T-},{A})}

where, for
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b\in\mathcal{B}
, the natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{A}}({Jb^{\prime}},{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{A}}({Jb^{\prime}},{A})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{A}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ Jf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{A}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{A}_{b^{\prime}}}

is defined, for each
h\colon Jb\to A
, the composite

\hbox to341.21pt{\vbox to21.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 170.60411pt\lower-9.11586pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-170.60411pt}{-2.95613pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

Indeed, the algebra axioms ensure that the requirements of Definition
3.1
are met (cf.
[
ASS25
, Lemma 3.22]
).

Now let
J,T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a pair of 2-functors. To motivate the appearance of oplax Kan extensions in the context of ’unrelativising’ algebras, consider the following situation. Generalising the notion of an algebra for an endofunctor, and in the light of Example
3.2
, we can define a
(
J
-relative) algebra
for
T
as an object
a\in\mathcal{A}
together with a lax transformation
(-)^{a}\colon{\mathcal{A}}({J-},{a})\Rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}({T-},{a})
. If a 2-functor
\widetilde{T}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is to have the same algebras as
T
, then the datum of such a lax transformation must correspond to that of a 1-cell
\widetilde{T}a\to a
in
\mathcal{A}
. Thus, we arrive at the following definition, which lifts that of an enriched left Kan extension from
[
Kel05
, §4.20]
to the lax setting.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
J,T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a pair of 2-functors. A 2-functor
\widetilde{T}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is a
left oplax Kan extension
of
T
along
J
, if, for any pair
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, there is an isomorphism of categories

{\mathcal{A}}({\widetilde{T}a},{a^{\prime}})\cong{\mathbf{Lax}\left[{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{op}},{\bf CAT}\right]}({{\mathcal{A}}({J-},{a})},{{\mathcal{A}}({T-},{a^{\prime}})})

which is natural in
a
and
a^{\prime}
.

At first glance, the above definition does not seem reminiscent of the familiar universal property of a left Kan extension from 1-dimensional category theory (see
[
Mac71
, §X.3]
). To recover an analogue of this property, and to justify the name ‘
oplax
Kan extension’ even though it is
lax
transformations which appear in Definition
3.3
, we need to speak of
lax ends
. The notion of a lax end was introduced in
[
Boz77
,
Boz80
]
, generalising ends to the lax setting. The theory of lax ends is then furthered in
[
Hir22
]
by developing a lax end/coend calculus parallel to the ordinary end/coend one (see e.g.
[
Lor21
]
). We refer the reader to Appendix
A
for the relevant definitions.

In terms of lax ends, by
[
Hir22
, §3.1]
, the left oplax Kan extension of
T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
along
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is defined, for each pair
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, by a natural isomorphism of categories

{\mathcal{A}}({\widetilde{T}a},{a^{\prime}})\cong\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-18.18416pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 18.18416pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-17.88904pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 17.88904pt}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{a}),{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{a^{\prime}})\right]

where the right-hand term is the oplax end of the 2-functor

{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}\qquad(b_{0},b_{1})\mapsto\left[{\mathcal{A}}({Jb_{1}},{a}),{\mathcal{A}}({Tb_{0}},{a^{\prime}})\right].

The lax end calculus of
[
Hir22
]
then allows us to deduce the following property of left oplax Kan extensions, involving
oplax
transformations, which generalises the universal property of a 1-dimensional Kan extension.

Proposition 3.4
.

If
\widetilde{T}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is a left oplax Kan extension of
T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
along
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
, then for each 2-functor
S\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
there is an isomorphism of categories:

\mathbf{Str}\left[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\right](\widetilde{T},S)\cong\mathbf{Oplax}\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}\right]\left(T,S\circ J\right)

which is natural in
S
.

Proof.

A proof of this is given in Proposition
A.4
.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

If
\widetilde{T}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is a left oplax Kan extension of
T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
along
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
, then there is an oplax transformation
\zeta\colon T\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}\circ J
such that, for every other 2-functor
S\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
together with an oplax transformation
\varepsilon\colon T\Rightarrow S\circ J
, there exists a unique strict transformation
\underline{\varepsilon}\colon\widetilde{T}\Rightarrow S
such that
\varepsilon=(\underline{\varepsilon}*J)\circ\zeta
, i.e. such that the natural transformation on the left coincides with the pasting on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\widetilde{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underline{\varepsilon}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Proof.

Take
\zeta
to be the unique oplax transformation corresponding to the strict transformation
\operatorname{id}\colon\widetilde{T}\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The property expressed by Corollary
3.5
makes it evident that left oplax Kan extensions are unique up to isomorphism whenever they exist.

3.2.
{\bf CAT}
-valued left oplax Kan extensions

In the case of
{\bf CAT}
-valued 2-functors, left oplax Kan extensions can be described explicitly. To provide intuition on how we will show this, recall from
[
Mac71
, §X]
that the (pointwise) left Kan extension
L\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf Set}
of a functor
T\colon D\to{\bf Set}
along a functor
J\colon D\to{\bf Set}
exists if and only if, for each set
X
, the coend
\int^{d\in D}{{\bf Set}}({Jd},{X})\times Td
is a set. In that case, this coend is the value of
L
at
X
, while the action of
L
on arrows is uniquely determined by the universal property of coends.
1
1

1

For a more general argument in the enriched case, see
[
Kel05
, §4.25]
.
An analogous relation holds between left oplax Kan extensions and oplax coends in
{\bf CAT}
: the left oplax Kan extension
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
of a 2-functor
T\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
along a 2-functor
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
is uniquely determined by setting
\widetilde{T}C
, for a category
C
, to be the oplax coend
\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int^{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int^{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int^{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-18.18416pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 18.18416pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int^{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-17.88904pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 17.88904pt}}\left[Jb,C\right]\times Tb
(Proposition
A.5
). In this section we will compute this oplax coend explicitly, obtaining a description of the left oplax Kan extensions of
T
along
J
.

Definition 3.7
.

Fix a pair of 2-functors
J,T\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
. For a category
C
, we let
\widetilde{T}C
be the following category.

(1)

An object of
\widetilde{T}C
is a triple
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)
consisting of an object
b\in\mathcal{B}
, a functor
h\colon Jb\to C
, and an object
\nu\in Tb
.

(2)

An arrow
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to C,\nu^{\prime})
in
\widetilde{T}C
is an equivalence class of triples
(f,\alpha,\varphi)
consisting of a 1-cell
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b
in
\mathcal{B}
, a natural transformation
\alpha\colon h\circ Jf\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
, and an arrow
\varphi\colon\nu\to Tf(\nu^{\prime})
in the category
Tb
, with obvious identities and compositions.

We identify two such triples
(f,\alpha_{f},\varphi_{f}),(g,\alpha_{g},\varphi_{g})\colon(b,h,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
up to the equivalence relation generated by letting
(f,\alpha_{f},\varphi_{f})\sim(g,\alpha_{g},\varphi_{g})
if there exists a 2-cell
\sigma\colon f\Rightarrow g
in
\mathcal{B}
such that

(a)

\alpha_{g}\circ(h*J\sigma)=\alpha_{f}
, i.e. the pasting on the left coincides with the natural transformation on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Jb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Jb,}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Jf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Jg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{g}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Jb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Jb;}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Jf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}}

(b)

and
\varphi_{g}=(T\sigma)_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ\varphi_{f}
, i.e. the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Tf(\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Tg(\nu^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\sigma)_{\nu^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}

commutes.

This assignment on categories extends to a 2-functor
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
. For a functor
F\colon C\to D
, we set
\widetilde{T}F\colon\widetilde{T}C\to\widetilde{T}D
to be the functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,F\circ h\colon Jb\to D,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b^{\prime},h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to C,\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b^{\prime},F\circ h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to C,\nu^{\prime})};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\alpha,\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,F*\alpha,\varphi)}

and for a natural transformation
\gamma\colon F\Rightarrow G
, we set
\widetilde{T}\gamma\colon\widetilde{T}F\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}G
to be the natural transformation whose component at
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}C
is the arrow

(\operatorname{id}_{b},\gamma\ast h,\operatorname{id}_{\nu})\colon(b,Fh\colon Jb\to D,\nu)\to(b,Gh\colon Jb\to D,\nu).

Notation 3.8
.

We will systematically abuse notation by identifying an equivalence class with one of its representatives where possible.

Remark 3.9
.

In our main example of weak ultracategories,
\mathcal{B}
will be the category
{\bf Set}
seen as a locally discrete 2-category. In this case, the datum of an arrow
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to C,\nu^{\prime})
in
\widetilde{T}C
reduces to a pair of a function
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b
such that
\nu=Tf(\nu^{\prime})
and a natural transformation
\alpha\colon h\circ Jf\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
. In particular, the equivalence relation in Definition
3.3
reduces to the identity.

Proposition 3.10
.

The 2-functor
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
of Definition
3.7
is the left oplax Kan extension of
T
along
J
.

Proof.

For a category
C
, a proof of the fact that the category
\widetilde{T}C
is the oplax coend of the 2-functor
{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
defined by
(b,b^{\prime})\mapsto\left[Jb,C\right]\times Tb
is in Proposition
A.6
. The 2-functor
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
described in Definition
3.7
is then precisely the unique extension of the assignment
C\mapsto\widetilde{T}C
induced by the universal properties of oplax coends, and hence it is the left oplax Kan extension of
T
along
J
(see Proposition
A.5
).
∎

Remark 3.11
.

By Proposition
3.4
,
\widetilde{T}
is equipped with a universal oplax transformation
\zeta\colon T\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}\circ J
, which is defined:

(1)

for an object
b\in\mathcal{B}
, by the functor
\zeta_{b}\colon Tb\to\widetilde{T}Jb
defined as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb}\colon Jb\to Jb,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb}\colon Jb\to Jb,\nu^{\prime});}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id}_{b},\operatorname{id},\varphi)}

(2)

for a 1-cell
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b\in\mathcal{B}
, by the natural transformation
\zeta_{f}\colon\zeta_{b}\circ Tf\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}Jf\circ\zeta_{b^{\prime}}
which, in turn, is defined at an object
\nu^{\prime}\in Tb^{\prime}
by the arrow

(f,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb}\colon Jb\to Jb,Tf(\nu^{\prime}))\to(b^{\prime},Jf\colon Jb^{\prime}\to Jb,\nu^{\prime})

in
\widetilde{T}Jb
.

In particular, every oplax transformation
\varepsilon\colon T\Rightarrow S\circ J
, for a 2-functor
S\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
factors uniquely through
\zeta
via a strict transformation
\underline{\varepsilon}\colon\widetilde{T}\Rightarrow S
, which we can describe explicitly. For each category
C
, its component
\underline{\varepsilon}_{C}\colon\widetilde{T}C\Rightarrow SC
is the functor defined:

(1)

on objects, by mapping
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)
to the image of
\nu\in Tb
under the functors
(Sh)\circ\varepsilon_{b}\colon Tb\to SJb\to SC
.

(2)

on arrows, by mapping
(f,\alpha,\varphi)\colon(b,h,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Sh\circ\varepsilon_{b})(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Sh\circ\varepsilon_{b}\circ Tf)(\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Sh\circ SJf\circ\varepsilon_{b^{\prime}})(\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Sh^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon_{b^{\prime}})(\nu^{\prime}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Sh\circ\varepsilon_{b})(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Sh*\varepsilon_{f})_{\nu^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S\alpha*\varepsilon_{b^{\prime}})_{\nu^{\prime}}}

Remark 3.12
.

By taking
{\bf CAT}
to be the (very large) 2-category of large categories, the above construction does not raise size issues. However, note that if
\mathcal{B}
is locally small as a 2-category and
Jb
is a small category for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
preserves local smallness, so that we can replace
{\bf CAT}
with the (large) 2-category of locally small categories.

4.
Unrelativising relative 2-monads

We now arrive at the main construction of the paper. Throughout,
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
is a fixed 2-functor and
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
is a fixed
J
-relative 2-monad. Moreover, we make the following assumptions:

(1)

\mathcal{B}
has a terminal object
1_{\mathcal{B}}
and it is preserved by
J
, where we use
\bullet
to denote the unique object in
J1_{\mathcal{B}}=1_{\bf CAT}
and
!=\operatorname{id}_{\bullet}
the unique arrow;

(2)

\mathcal{B}
admits all oplax colimits for diagrams of shape
{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}
for
b\in\mathcal{B}
(see Definition
4.3
below) and they are preserved by
J
;

(3)

J
is 2-fully faithful.

Under these assumptions, we will show that the left oplax Kan extension
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
of
T
along
J
constructed in the previous section carries the structure of a
pseudomonad
(see e.g.
[
Lac00
]
for a definition including coherence axioms). Later, in Section
5
, we will show that algebras for
T
coincide with algebras for
\widetilde{T}
with respect to this pseudomonad structure.

Remark 4.1
.

Note that condition (2) is automatically satisfied under the stronger hypotheses that
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
factors through small categories,
\mathcal{B}
admits all small oplax colimits and these are preserved by
J
.

With the aim of turning a relative monad with root
J\colon D\to C
into an ordinary monad on
C
, in
[
ACU15
]
, the authors endow the category
\left[D,C\right]
with a
skew-monoidal
structure in the sense of
[
Szl12
]
, hence identifying
J
-relative monads with
skew-monoids
in
\left[D,C\right]
. This draws a parallel with the familiar case of (ordinary) monads on a category
C
, which are monoids in the monoidal category
\left[C,C\right]
. In the case of a “well-behaved” root functor in the sense of
[
ACU15
, Definition 4.1]
, this skew-monoidal structure is then proved to be actually monoidal, which is exploited to define a monad on
C
with the same category of algebras.

In this section, we will proceed similarly. We will begin by endowing the category
\mathbf{Oplax}\left[\mathcal{B},{\bf CAT}\right]
, where our relative 2-monad lives, with a ‘
J
-relative’ composition operation making it into a skew-monoidal category. The skew-monoidal structure will be defined essentially by making use of the universal properties of left oplax Kan extensions. In contrast to
[
ACU15
]
, by exploiting the above assumptions on
J
we will define additional structure for the
J
-relative composition, which we will use to endow
\widetilde{T}
with the structure of a pseudomonad. Crucially, our assumptions – which are not direct generalisations of the “well-behavedness” conditions of
[
ACU15
, Definition 4.1]
– will not be strong enough to make the skew-monoidal structure actually monoidal (see Remark
4.8
).

Remark 4.2
.

The operation of taking left oplax Kan extensions along
J
is functorial on
\mathbf{Oplax}\left[\mathcal{B},{\bf CAT}\right]
, where given a pair of 2-functors
F,G\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
and an oplax transformation
\sigma\colon F\Rightarrow G
, we define
\widetilde{\sigma}\colon\widetilde{F}\Rightarrow\widetilde{G}
as the unique strict transformation universally induced by the the oplax transformation
\zeta^{G}\circ\sigma\colon F\Rightarrow G\Rightarrow\widetilde{G}\circ F
.

In particular, when
\sigma
is a
strict
transformation, its component at a category
C
is the functor
\widetilde{\sigma}_{C}\colon\widetilde{F}(C)\to\widetilde{G}(C)
defined as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu\in Fb)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\sigma_{b}(\nu)\in Gb)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b^{\prime},h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to C,\nu^{\prime}\in Fb^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b^{\prime},h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to C,\sigma_{b^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})\in Gb^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\alpha,\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\alpha,\sigma_{b}(\varphi))}

which is well-defined since
\sigma_{b}(Ff(\nu^{\prime}))=Gf(\sigma_{b^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime}))
.

4.1.
Oplax colimits

As we will make extensive use of the universal properties of oplax colimits, we recall here their definition for the convenience of the reader.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a 2-category,
I
a (1-)category, and
F\colon I\to\mathcal{A}
a (1-)functor.

(1)

A
(strict) oplax cocone
for the diagram
F
consists of the data of

(a)

an object
\ell\in\mathcal{A}
called the
vertex
,

(b)

1-cells
c_{i}\colon Fi\to\ell
for each
i\in I
called the
coprojections
,

(c)

and 2-cells
\lambda_{m}\colon c_{j}\circ Fm\Rightarrow c_{i}
for each
m\colon i\to j\in I

satisfying
\lambda_{\operatorname{id}_{i}}=\operatorname{id}_{c_{i}}
and
\lambda_{m}\circ(\lambda_{n}\ast Fm)=\lambda_{n\circ m}
for each composable pair of arrows
m\colon i\to j,n\colon j\to k\in I
, i.e. the pasting on the left coincides with the natural transformation on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fm}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fn}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{n}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fm}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fn}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{n\circ m}}

(2)

A
(strict) oplax colimit
for the diagram
F
is an oplax cocone
(\ell,c_{i},\lambda_{m})_{i,m\in I}
for
F
with the following universal properties.

(a)

For any other oplax cocone
(k,d_{i},\kappa_{m})_{i,m\in I}
for
F
, there exists a unique 1-cell
g\colon\ell\to k
such that
g\circ c_{i}=d_{i}
and
g\ast\lambda_{m}=\kappa_{m}
for each
m\colon i\to j\in I
.

(b)

Moreover, given any other 1-cell
h\colon\ell\to k
and 2-cells
\beta_{i}\colon h\circ c_{i}\Rightarrow g\circ c_{i}=d_{i}
such that
(g\ast\lambda_{m})\circ(\beta_{j}\ast Fm)=\beta_{i}\circ(h\ast\lambda_{m})
for each
m\colon i\to j\in I
, i.e. the pastings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fm}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{m}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fm}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{m}}

coincide for each
m\colon i\to j\in I
, then there exists a unique 2-cell
\beta\colon h\Rightarrow g
such that
\beta_{i}=\beta\ast c_{i}
.

Remark 4.4
.

As with ordinary colimits, note that if
k\in\mathcal{A}
is (the vertex of) an oplax cocone for a diagram
F\colon I\to\mathcal{A}
for which
\ell_{0},\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
are both (vertices of) oplax colimits, so that in particular there exists a canonical isomorphism
f\colon\ell_{0}\to\ell_{1}
, then the uniquely determined 1-cells
g_{0}\colon\ell_{0}\to k
,
g_{1}\colon\ell_{1}\to k
satisfy
g_{1}\circ f=g_{0}
.

Example 4.5
.

Let
I
be a category, and let
R\colon{I}^{\operatorname{op}}\to{\bf CAT}
be the functor that sends each object of
I
to the terminal category
1_{\bf CAT}
and each morphism of
I
to the identity on
1_{\bf CAT}
. In this case, the oplax colimit is given by
I
itself, with the corresponding 1-cell of the cocone
c_{i}\colon 1_{\bf CAT}\to I
at an object
i\in{I}^{\operatorname{op}}
given by the functor that picks out the object
i\in I
, while the 2-cell
\lambda_{m}\colon c_{j}\circ\operatorname{id}_{1_{\bf CAT}}\Rightarrow c_{i}
at an arrow
m\colon i\to j\in{I}^{\operatorname{op}}
is the natural transformation whose (only) component is
m\colon j\to i\in I
.

4.2.
Skew-monoidal structure

We define the
J
-relative composition
operation

\circ^{J}\colon{\mathbf{Oplax}}\left[\mathcal{B},{\bf CAT}\right]\times{\mathbf{Oplax}}\left[\mathcal{B},{\bf CAT}\right]\to{\mathbf{Oplax}}\left[\mathcal{B},{\bf CAT}\right]

by letting, on objects,
F\circ^{J}G\coloneqq\widetilde{F}\circ G
, which clearly extends to a functor making use of Remark
4.2
. This operation can be endowed with the following structural maps.

(1)

We define a
left unit
, a family of oplax transformations
\mathfrak{l}_{F}\colon J\circ^{J}F\Rightarrow F
for each 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
, natural in
F
, as follows.

First, consider the strict transformation
\operatorname{id}_{J}\colon J\Rightarrow J
. By the universal property of
\widetilde{J}
, it uniquely factors through
\zeta^{J}\colon J\Rightarrow\widetilde{J}\circ J
via a strict transformation
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}\colon\widetilde{J}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{{\bf CAT}}
, from which we define
\mathfrak{l}_{F}\coloneq\bar{\mathfrak{l}}*F
. Explicitly, this means that for each object
c\in\mathcal{B}
, the component
(\mathfrak{l}_{F})_{c}\colon\widetilde{J}(C)\to C
is the functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,h\colon Jb\to Fc,x\in Jb)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b^{\prime},h^{\prime}\colon Jb^{\prime}\to Fc,x^{\prime}\in Jb^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime}(x^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\alpha,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x^{\prime}}\circ h(g)}

(2)

We define a
right unit
, a family of oplax transformations
\mathfrak{r}_{F}\colon F\Rightarrow F\circ^{J}J
for each 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
, natural in
F
, by considering for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
the functor

(\mathfrak{r}_{F})_{b}\coloneqq(\iota_{F,Jb})_{b}(\operatorname{id}_{Jb})\colon Fb\to\widetilde{F}(Jb)

explicitly defined as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb}\colon Jb\to Jb,\nu\in Fb)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nu^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb}\colon Jb\to Jb,\nu^{\prime}\in Fb)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id}_{b},\operatorname{id},\varphi)}

and, for each 1-cell
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b\in\mathcal{B}
, the natural transformation
(\mathfrak{r}_{F})_{f}\colon(\mathfrak{r}_{F})_{b}\circ Ff\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}Jf\circ(\mathfrak{r}_{F})_{b^{\prime}}
defined at an object
\nu^{\prime}\in Fb^{\prime}
by the arrow

((\mathfrak{r}_{F})_{f})_{\nu^{\prime}}\coloneqq(f,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb},Ff(\nu^{\prime}))\to(b^{\prime},Jf,\nu^{\prime})

in
\widetilde{F}(Jb)
.

(3)

We define an
associator
, a family of oplax transformations
\mathfrak{a}_{F,G,H}\colon(F\circ^{J}G)\circ^{J}H\Rightarrow F\circ^{J}(G\circ^{J}H)
for each triple of 2-functors
F,G,H\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
, natural in
F
,
G
and
H
, as follows.

First, consider the oplax transformation
\widetilde{F}*\zeta^{G}\colon\widetilde{F}\circ G\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}\circ\widetilde{G}\circ J
. By the universal property of
\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ G}
, it uniquely factors through
\zeta^{\widetilde{F}\circ G}\colon\widetilde{F}\circ G\Rightarrow\widetilde{(\widetilde{F}\circ G)}\circ J
via a strict transformation
\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{F,G}\colon\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ G}\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}\circ\widetilde{G}
, from which we define
\mathfrak{a}_{F,G,H}\coloneqq\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{F,G}*H
. Explicitly, this means that for each object
c\in\mathcal{B}
, the component
({\mathfrak{a}}_{F,G,H})_{c}\colon\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ G}(Hc)\to\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{G}(Hc))
is the functor defined:

(a)

on objects, by mapping
(b,h\colon Jb\to Hc,(d,k\colon Jd\to Gb,\nu\in Fd))
to the triple
(d,K\colon Jd\to\widetilde{G}(Hc),\nu\in Fd)
where
K
is the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Jd}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}(Jb)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}(Hc)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{G}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}(h)}

i.e. such that
K(x)\coloneqq(b,h\colon Jb\to Hc,k(x)\in Gb)
;

(b)

on arrows, by mapping
(f,\alpha,(g,\beta,\psi))\colon(b,h,(d,k,\nu))\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime},(d^{\prime},k^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}))
to the triple of
g\colon d^{\prime}\to d\in\mathcal{B}
, the natural transformation
Jg\circ K\Rightarrow K^{\prime}
given by the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Jd}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}(Jb)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}(Hc)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Jd^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gb^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{G}(Jb^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{G}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{G}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Jg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{G}_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}(Jf)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}(h^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}(\alpha)}

and
\psi\colon\nu\to Fg(\nu^{\prime})\in Fd
.

Remark 4.6
.

Note how the transformations
\mathfrak{l}_{F}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{F,G,H}
are actually strict rather than merely oplax.

Proposition 4.7
.

The category
\mathbf{Oplax}\left[\mathcal{B},{\bf CAT}\right]
, together with the composition
\circ^{J}
and the families
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{a}
, is a skew-monoidal category.

Proof.

The proof follows exactly as in
[
ACU15
, Theorem 3.1]
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

In
[
ACU15
]
, the hypotheses of “well-behavedness” on the root functor ensures that the families
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{a}
are invertible, i.e. that the skew-monoidal structure is monoidal. If our assumptions on the root 2-functor were to imply the same, our motivating example of weak ultracategories would be left out. For instance, it is easy to see that
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}
is not invertible for
J\colon{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
being the inclusion of sets as discrete categories: in other words, the left oplax Kan extension of
J
along itself is not (isomorphic to) the identity. In a way, our assumptions on
J
are intended to do without these inverses.

In light of the previous remark, we now use our assumptions on
\mathcal{B}
and on the root
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
to define additional structure for the
J
-relative composition.

(4)

We define a strict transformation
\mathfrak{d}\colon\operatorname{id}_{{\bf CAT}}\Rightarrow\widetilde{J}
by setting
\mathfrak{d}_{C}\colon C\to\widetilde{J}(C)
, for each category
C
, to be the functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{\mathcal{B}},c\colon 1_{{\bf CAT}}\to C,\bullet)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{\mathcal{B}},c^{\prime}\colon 1_{{\bf CAT}}\to C,\bullet)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id},f,\operatorname{id})}

where by assumption
J1_{\mathcal{B}}=1_{{\bf CAT}}
is the terminal category. Clearly,
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}\circ\mathfrak{d}=\operatorname{id}
.

(5)

For each pair of 2-functors
F,G\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
and for each category
C
, let

(\mathfrak{s}_{F,G})_{C}\colon\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{G}(C))\to\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ G}(C)

be the functor defined as follows.

Consider an object
(b,h\colon Jb\to\widetilde{G}C,\nu\in Fb)\in\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{G}C)
. For each object
x\in Jb
and each arrow
g\colon x\to y\in Jb
, we will use the following notation to denote their image under
h
:

{h(x)\coloneqq(Rx,a_{x}\colon JRx\to C,\nu_{x}\in GRx)},\quad{{h(g)\coloneqq\left(Rg,\gamma_{g},\psi_{g}\right)}}.

Note that this yields a functor
R\coloneqq\pi\circ{h}^{\operatorname{op}}\colon{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
. Then, we define the action of
(\mathfrak{s}_{F,G})_{C}
on
(b,h,\nu)
as the tuple
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(b,Q\colon Jb\to G\ell,\nu\in Fb))
where:

(a)

\ell\in\mathcal{B}
is the oplax colimit of the diagram
R
, witnessed by families

\set{c_{x}\colon Rx\to\ell}{x\in Jb}\mbox{ and }\set{\lambda_{g}\colon c_{x}\circ Rg\Rightarrow c_{y}}{g\colon x\to y\in Jb},

(b)

a\colon J\ell\to C
is the functor uniquely induced by seeing
C
as an oplax cocone on the diagram
J\circ R
through the families

\set{a_{x}\colon JRx\to C}{x\in Jb}\mbox{ and }\set{\gamma_{g}\colon a_{x}\circ JRg\Rightarrow a_{y}}{g\colon x\to y\in Jb},

(c)

and
Q\colon Jb\to G\ell
is the functor extending
x\mapsto Gc_{x}(\nu_{x})
by setting
Qg
, for an arrow
g\colon x\to y\in Jb
, to be the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gc_{x}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gc_{x}(GRg(\nu_{y}))=G(c_{x}\circ Rg)(\nu_{y})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gc_{y}(\nu_{y}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gc_{x}(\psi_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\lambda_{g})_{\nu_{y}}}

Now consider an arrow
(f,\alpha,\varphi)\colon(b,h,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\in\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{G}C)
. Building on the previous notation, we will denote the component of
\alpha\colon h\circ Jf\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
at
x\in Jb^{\prime}
as the triple

\left(w_{x},\delta_{x},\chi_{x}\right)\colon{h(Jf(x))=(RJf(x),a_{Jf(x)},\nu_{Jf(x)})}\to{(R^{\prime}x,a_{x}^{\prime},\nu_{x}^{\prime})=h^{\prime}(x)}.

Then, we define the action of
(\mathfrak{s}_{F,G})_{C}
on
(f,\alpha,\varphi)
as the tuple
\left(\bar{f},\bar{\alpha},(f,\theta,\varphi)\right)
where:

(a)

\bar{f}\colon\ell^{\prime}\to\ell\in\mathcal{B}
is the 1-cell uniquely induced by seeing
\ell
as an oplax cocone on
R^{\prime}
through the families

\set{c_{Jf(x)}\circ w_{x}}{x\in Jb^{\prime}}\mbox{ and }\set{\lambda_{Jf(g)}*w_{y}}{g\colon x\to y\in Jb^{\prime}},

(b)

\bar{\alpha}\colon a\circ\bar{f}\Rightarrow a^{\prime}
is the natural transformation uniquely induced by the universal property of
J\ell^{\prime}
as the oplax colimit of
J\circ R^{\prime}
through the family

\set{\delta_{x}\colon a\circ f^{F}\circ Jc^{\prime}_{x}\Rightarrow a^{\prime}_{x}}{x\in Jb^{\prime}},

(c)

and
\theta\colon Q\circ Jf\Rightarrow G\bar{f}\circ Q^{\prime}
is the natural transformation defined at
x\in Jb^{\prime}
by the arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces QJf(x)=Gc_{Jf(x)}(\nu_{Jf(x)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gc_{Jf(x)}(Gw_{x}(\nu_{x}^{\prime}))}={G\bar{f}(Gc_{x}^{\prime}(\nu_{x}^{\prime}))}={G\bar{f}(Q^{\prime}x).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gc_{Jf(x)}(\chi_{x})}

We omit the routine verification that the above functors are well-defined on equivalence classes and define a strict transformation

\mathfrak{s}_{F,G}\colon\widetilde{F}\circ\widetilde{G}\Rightarrow\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ G}
, and that the family
\set{\mathfrak{s}_{F,G}}{F,G\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}}
is natural in both arguments.

Proposition 4.9
.

For each 2-functor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
, the following diagrams of strict transformations commute up to a canonical invertible modification:

(a)\quad\hbox to133.59pt{\vbox to48.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 66.79411pt\lower-23.74165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.72197pt}{-27.61581pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
