\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Proof.

We consider each diagram in turn.

(a)

Let
C
be a category and let
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu\in Fb)\in\widetilde{F}C
, which
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}*F})_{C}
maps to the triple
(b,h,\mathfrak{d}_{Fb}(\nu))
i.e.
(b,h,(1_{\mathcal{B}},\nu\colon 1_{{\bf CAT}}\to Fb,\bullet))
. On the other side,
\mathfrak{d}_{\widetilde{F}C}
maps
(b,h,\nu)
to the tuple
(1_{\mathcal{B}},(b,h,\nu)\colon 1_{{\bf CAT}}\to\widetilde{F}C,\bullet)
, which is then sent by
(\mathfrak{s}_{J,F})_{C}
to a tuple
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(1_{\mathcal{B}},Q\colon 1_{{\bf CAT}}\to F\ell,\bullet))
, where by definition
\ell\in\mathcal{B}
is the oplax colimit of the diagram
R\colon{(1_{{\bf CAT}})}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
that picks out the object
b\in\mathcal{B}
. Evidently,
b
itself is an oplax colimit of
R
, so there is a canonically induced invertible 1-cell
u\colon b\to\ell\in\mathcal{B}
yielding the invertible arrow:

(u,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon(\ell,a,(1_{\mathcal{B}},Q,\bullet))\to(b,h,(1_{\mathcal{B}},\nu,\bullet))

in
(\widetilde{\widetilde{J}\circ F})(C)
, which is the component of an invertible modification
\mathfrak{s}_{J,F}\circ(\mathfrak{d}*\widetilde{F})\Rrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}*F}
.

(b)

Let
C
be a category and let
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu\in Fb)\in\widetilde{F}C
, which
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}_{F}})_{C}
maps to the triple
(b,h,(\mathfrak{r}_{F})_{b}(\nu))
i.e.
(b,h,(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb},\nu))
. On the other side,
\widetilde{F}(\mathfrak{d}_{C})
maps
(b,h,\nu)
to the triple
(b,\mathfrak{d}_{C}\circ h,\nu)
, which
(\mathfrak{s}_{F,J})_{C}
then maps to a tuple
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(b,Q\colon Jb\to J\ell,\nu))
where by definition
\ell\in\mathcal{B}
is the oplax colimit of the constant diagram
R\colon{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
of value
1_{\mathcal{B}}
. By Example
4.5
and 2-fully-faithfulness of
J
,
b\in\mathcal{B}
is also an oplax colimit of
R
, so there is a canonically induced invertible 1-cell
v\colon b\to\ell\in\mathcal{B}
yielding the invertible arrow:

(v,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon(\ell,a,(b,Q,\nu))\to(b,h,(b,\operatorname{id}_{Jb},\nu))

in
(\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ J})(C)
, which is the component of an invertible modification
\mathfrak{s}_{F,J}\circ(\widetilde{F}*\mathfrak{d})\Rrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}_{F}}
.

(c)

Let
C
be a category and let
(b,h\colon Jb\to\widetilde{F}^{2}C,\nu\in Fb)\in\widetilde{F}^{3}C
. By definition,
\widetilde{F}((\mathfrak{s}_{F,F})_{C})
maps it to the triple
(b,(\mathfrak{s}_{F,F})_{C}\circ h,\nu)
, which
(\mathfrak{s}_{F,\widetilde{F}\circ F})_{C}
then maps to a tuple
(\bar{\ell},\bar{a}\colon J\bar{\ell}\to C,(b,\bar{Q}\colon Jb\to\widetilde{F}(F\bar{\ell}),\nu))
where

\bar{\ell}\coloneqq\opcolim_{x\in{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\opcolim_{y\in{(JRx)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a_{x}(y)\right)\right)

Since oplax colimits commute with oplax colimits (cf.
[
Bon24
]
), there is a canonical isomorphism:

\opcolim_{x\in{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\opcolim_{y\in{(JRx)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a_{x}(y)\right)\right)\cong\opcolim_{(x,y)\in{\left(\opcolim_{x^{\prime}\in{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}}(JRx^{\prime})\right)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a_{x}(y)\right)

where, in the right-hand side,
(x,y)
denotes the image of
y\in JRx
along the coprojection functor
JRx\to\opcolim_{x^{\prime}\in{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}}(JRx^{\prime})
.
Chasing
(b,h,\nu)
along the other side of the diagram, instead, we have that:

(1)

(\mathfrak{s}_{F,F})_{\widetilde{F}C}
maps it to the tuple

(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to\widetilde{F}C,(b,Q\colon Jb\to F\ell,\nu))

where
\ell\in\mathcal{B}
is the oplax colimit of the diagram
R\colon{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
– with coprojections
\set{c_{x}\colon Rx\to\ell}{x\in Jb}
;

(2)

(\mathfrak{s}_{\widetilde{F}\circ F,F})_{C}
maps (1) to the tuple

(\ell^{\prime},a^{\prime}\colon J\ell^{\prime}\to C,(\ell,Q^{\prime}\colon J\ell\to F\ell^{\prime},(b,Q\colon Jb\to F\ell,\nu)))

where
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
is the oplax colimit of the diagram
\pi\circ{a}^{\operatorname{op}}\colon{(J\ell)}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
;

(3)

(\widetilde{\bar{{\mathfrak{a}}}_{F,F}*F})_{C}
maps (2) to the tuple

(\ell^{\prime},a^{\prime}\colon J\ell^{\prime}\to C,(\bar{{\mathfrak{a}}}_{F,F})_{F\ell^{\prime}}(\ell,Q^{\prime}\colon J\ell\to F\ell^{\prime},(b,Q\colon Jb\to F\ell,\nu)))

i.e.
(\ell^{\prime},a^{\prime}\colon J\ell^{\prime}\to C,(b,K\colon Jb\to\widetilde{F}(F\ell^{\prime}),\nu))
, where
K\coloneqq\widetilde{F}(Q^{\prime})\circ\zeta_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ Q
.

Note therefore that, by our assumptions,
J\ell
is the oplax colimit of
J\circ R
, with coprojections
\set{Jc_{x}\colon JRx\to J\ell}{x\in Jb}
. Therefore, recalling also that
a\circ Jc_{x}=a_{x}
by definition of
a\colon J\ell\to\widetilde{F}C
, we have that:

\displaystyle\opcolim_{(x,y)\in{\left(\opcolim_{x^{\prime}\in{(Jb)}^{\operatorname{op}}}(JRx^{\prime})\right)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a_{x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\opcolim_{z=Jc_{x}(y)\in{(J\ell)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a_{x}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\opcolim_{z=Jc_{x}(y)\in{(J\ell)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a(Jc_{x}(y))\right)

\displaystyle=\opcolim_{z\in{(J\ell)}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\pi a(z)\right)=\ell^{\prime},

and hence there is a canonically induced invertible 1-cell
w\colon\ell^{\prime}\to\bar{\ell}\in\mathcal{B}
yielding the invertible arrow:

(w,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon(\bar{\ell},\bar{a},(b,\bar{Q},\nu))\to(\ell^{\prime},a^{\prime},(b,K,\nu)).

in
(\widetilde{\widetilde{F}^{2}\circ F})(C)
, which is the component of an invertible modification

\mathfrak{s}_{F,\widetilde{F}\circ F}\circ(\widetilde{F}*\mathfrak{s}_{F,F})\Rrightarrow\widetilde{\bar{{\mathfrak{a}}}_{F,F}*F}\circ\mathfrak{s}_{\widetilde{F}\circ F,F}\circ(\mathfrak{s}_{F,F}*\widetilde{F}).

∎

Remark 4.10
.

Given 2-functors
F,G,H\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
, the same proof of (c) can be generalised to show that a similar diagram going from
\widetilde{F}\circ\widetilde{G}\circ\widetilde{H}
to
\widetilde{\widetilde{F}\circ\widetilde{G}\circ H}
also commutes up to a canonical invertible modification, but we will not need this in the rest of the paper.

4.3.
Pseudomonad structure

We are finally ready to endow
\widetilde{T}
with the structure of a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
, that is, a unit
\eta^{\sharp}
and a multiplication
\mu^{\sharp}
together with appropriate coherent invertible modifications expressing unitality and associativity. Following
[
ACU15
]
, we first define a ‘
J
-relative multiplication’ for
T
– i.e. a strict transformation
\mu\colon T\circ^{J}T\Rightarrow T
– by considering, for each
b\in B
, the functor
\mu_{b}\colon\widetilde{T}(Tb)\to Tb
uniquely corresponding to the strict transformation
(-)^{*}\colon\left[J-,Tb\right]\Rightarrow\left[T-,Tb\right]
by the universal property of the left oplax Kan extension. Explicitly,
\mu_{b}
acts as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,k\colon Jd\to Tb,\nu\in Td)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d^{\prime},k^{\prime}\colon Jd^{\prime}\to Tb^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}\in Td^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{\prime*}(\nu^{\prime}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\alpha,\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ k^{*}(\varphi)}

(c)\quad\hbox to177.36pt{\vbox to87.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 88.87921pt\lower-42.93324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Definition 4.12
.

We endow
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
with the following pseudomonad structure.

(1)

First, we define a unit
\eta^{\sharp}\colon\operatorname{id}_{{\bf CAT}}\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}
as the strict transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{id}_{{\bf CAT}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}}

(2)

Second, we define a multiplication
\mu^{\sharp}\colon\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}
as the strict transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}

(3)

We define a
left unit

\mathfrak{p}\colon\mu^{\sharp}\circ(\eta^{\sharp}*\widetilde{T})\Rrightarrow\operatorname{id}
as the invertible modification obtained by pasting:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{J}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{J}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}*\widetilde{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{J,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\widetilde{\eta}*T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{d}*\widetilde{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}*T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(4)

We define a
right unit

\mathfrak{q}\colon\operatorname{id}\Rrightarrow\mu^{\sharp}\circ(\widetilde{T}*\eta^{\sharp})
as the invertible modification obtained by pasting:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}*\widetilde{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{s}_{T,J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}*\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}*\mathfrak{d}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}_{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(5)

Finally, we define an
associator

\mathfrak{x}\colon\mu^{\sharp}\circ(\widetilde{T}*\mu^{\sharp})\Rrightarrow\mu^{\sharp}\circ(\mu^{\sharp}*\widetilde{T})
as the invertible modification obtained by pasting:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\circ\widetilde{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\widetilde{T}\circ T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}*\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}*\widetilde{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}*\widetilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{T,\widetilde{T}\circ T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{\widetilde{T}\circ T,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}*\widetilde{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\widetilde{T}*\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,T}*T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}*T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Theorem 4.13
.

The triple
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
, together with the modifications
\mathfrak{p}
,
\mathfrak{q}
, and
\mathfrak{x}
, is a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
.

Proof.

The two coherence conditions spelled out in
[
Lac00
]
are satisfied since all arrows describing them are uniquely induced by the universal properties of oplax colimits.
∎

5.
Preservation of algebras

In the previous section, we constructed the pseudomonad
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
obtained by unrelativising a
J
-relative 2-monad
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
. This section is dedicated to proving that their colax algebras coincide. Before formally stating the theorem, we here recall the definitions regarding colax algebras for a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
. For practical purposes, we restrict ourselves to the context of the pseudomonad
\widetilde{T}
: that is, in the equational axioms, we will assume that the underlying pseudofunctor is actually a 2-functor rather than a pseudofunctor, and that the unit and the multiplication are strict transformations rather than pseudonatural transformations. For a more general treatment, we refer the reader to
[
Št24
]
.

Definition 5.1

(
[
Str74
, §2]
,
[
Št24
, §2.4]
)
.

A
colax algebra
for
\widetilde{T}
is a tuple
\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}
consisting of:

(1)

a category
C
;

(2)

a functor
\Phi\colon\widetilde{T}C\to C
;

(3)

a
counitor

\mathfrak{i}\colon\Phi\circ\eta_{C}^{\sharp}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{C}
;

(4)

a
coassociator

\mathfrak{m}\colon\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C}\Rightarrow\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi
,

such that:

(a)

both of the following pastings coincide with the identity transformation on
\Phi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta^{\sharp}_{\widetilde{T}C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta^{\sharp}_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{p}_{C}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C;}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\mathfrak{m}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\widetilde{T}\eta^{\sharp}_{C}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\widetilde{T}\Phi}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\mathfrak{q}_{C}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\widetilde{T}\mathfrak{i}}}}}

(b)

the following pastings coincide:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{3}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C,}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{x}_{C}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\sharp}_{\widetilde{T}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}^{2}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}\mathfrak{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{3}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\sharp}_{\widetilde{T}C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}^{2}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

A
colax morphism

\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\colon\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}\to\braket{C^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\mathfrak{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
of colax algebras for
\widetilde{T}
consists of:

(1)

a functor
U\colon C\to C^{\prime}
;

(2)

a natural transformation
\mathfrak{u}\colon U\circ\Phi\Rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{T}U
,

such that:

(a)

the following pastings coincide:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{C}^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{C^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime};}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{C}^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}}}

(b)

the following pastings coincide:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime},}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathfrak{u}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\mathfrak{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\widetilde{T}U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\Phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Phi^{\prime}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu^{\sharp}_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}^{2}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu^{\sharp}_{C^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\Phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Phi^{\prime}}}}

A
lax morphism
is defined analogously, reversing the direction of
\mathfrak{u}
. In particular, a (co)lax morphism
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}
is a
pseudomorphism
if
\mathfrak{u}
is invertible, and it is a
strict morphism
if
\mathfrak{u}
is the identity.

Let
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}},\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}\colon\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}\to\braket{C^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\mathfrak{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
be colax morphisms of colax algebras for
\widetilde{T}
. A
transformation

\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
is a natural transformation
\alpha\colon U\Rightarrow U^{\prime}
such that the following pastings coincide:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}\alpha}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{T}U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Transformations between lax morphisms are defined analogously.

Colax algebras, their morphisms and transformations form 2-categories:

(1)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})
, with colax morphisms;

(2)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{lax}(\widetilde{T})
, with lax morphisms;

(3)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{ps}(\widetilde{T})
, with pseudomorphisms;

(4)

{\rm ColaxAlg}_{str}(\widetilde{T})
, with strict morphisms.

Notation 5.2
.

As in Notation
1.6
, we will write
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}(\widetilde{T})
for
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}
to refer to any of the above 2-categories.

Remark 5.3
.

Analogous to Example
1.2
, the pseudomonad
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
can be viewed as a relative pseudomonad over the identity
\operatorname{id}_{\bf CAT}
in the sense of
[
FGHW18
]
. Thus, there is another notion for the colax algebras of
\widetilde{T}
given by generalising Definition
1.4
to the relative pseudomonad setting, (see
[
ASS25
]
or
[
MW13
, §4]
). The resultant 2-category of colax algebras is, however, bi-equivalent to the 2-category
{\rm ColaxAlg}(\widetilde{T})
described above (cfr.
[
MW13
, Theorem 5.1]
).

Theorem 5.4

(Unrelativisation preserves colax algebras)
.

Given a
J
-relative 2-monad
T
on
{\bf CAT}
satisfying the assumptions of Section
4
, for each
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}
the 2-categories
{\rm ColaxAlg}^{J}_{\star}(T)
and
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}(\widetilde{T})
are isomorphic.

To prove Theorem
5.4
, we will make extensive use of the universal property of
\widetilde{T}
as a left oplax Kan extension. In particular, we will show that the datum of a colax algebra for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
on a category
C
coincides with that of a colax algebra for
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
on the same category
C
. Indeed – recalling Example
3.2
and the discussion before Definition
3.3
– the former consists of a lax transformation
\left[J-,C\right]\Rightarrow\left[T-,C\right]
with extra structure, while the latter consists of a functor
\widetilde{T}C\to C
with extra structure. By Proposition
3.10
, such lax transformations and such functors are in bijection, hence Theorem
5.4
will follow by showing that the rest of the structure is also interdefinable. We present the construction for colax morphisms, but this is easily adapted.

5.1.
From
T
-algebras to
\widetilde{T}
-algebras

To witness the isomorphisms in Theorem
5.4
, we first build a 2-functor
\Theta\colon{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})
.

5.1.1.
On algebras

Let
\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}
be a colax algebra for the
J
-relative 2-monad
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
. We define a colax algebra

\Theta(\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta})\coloneqq\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}

for the pseudomonad
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
as follows.

By Example
3.2
, recall that the family of extension operators defines a lax transformation
(-)^{C}\colon\left[J-,C\right]\Rightarrow\left[T-,C\right]
, so we let the structure functor
\Phi\colon\widetilde{T}C\to C
be the uniquely induced functor by Proposition
3.10
. Explicitly, this means that
\Phi
is defined

(1)

on objects by mapping
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}C
to
h^{C}(\nu)
,

(2)

and on arrows by mapping
(f,\alpha,\varphi)\colon(b,h,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\in\widetilde{T}C
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}Tf(\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}Jf)^{C}(\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hJf)^{C}(\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime C}(\nu^{\prime}).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(\varphi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jf})_{\nu^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Gamma_{h}*Jf)^{C}_{\nu^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{C}_{\nu^{\prime}}}}

Next we define the counitor
\mathfrak{i}\colon\Phi\circ\eta_{C}^{\sharp}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{C}
. First note that
\Phi\circ\eta_{C}^{\sharp}\colon C\to C
is the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{C}_{\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet)}}

where we identify
c,c^{\prime}\in C
with functors
J1_{\mathcal{B}}=1_{{\bf CAT}}\to C
and hence
f\colon c\to c^{\prime}\in C
with a natural transformation
c\Rightarrow c^{\prime}
. Therefore, we define the component of
\mathfrak{i}
at an object
c\in C
as the arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\eta_{C}^{\sharp})(c)=c^{C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{c})_{\bullet}}

in
C
. Naturality of
\mathfrak{i}
follows immediately by that of the family
\set{\Gamma}
.

Finally, we define the coassociator
\mathfrak{m}\colon\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C}\Rightarrow\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi
. First note that the functor
\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C}\colon\widetilde{T}^{2}C\to C
acts:

(1)

on objects, by mapping
(b,h\colon Jb\to\widetilde{T}C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}^{2}C
to
a^{C}Q^{*}(\nu)\in C
, where we let
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(b,Q\colon Jb\to T\ell,\nu))\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}(b,h,\nu)
as in
4.2
;

(2)

on arrows, by mapping
(f,\alpha,\varphi)\colon(b,h,\nu)\to(b^{\prime},h^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\in\widetilde{T}^{2}C
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}Q^{*}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}T\bar{f}(Q^{\prime*}\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}\eta_{\ell}J\bar{f})^{C}(Q^{\prime*}\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(aJ\bar{f})^{C}(Q^{\prime*}\nu^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime C}Q^{\prime*}(\nu^{\prime}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}(\theta^{*}_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ Q^{*}(\varphi))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{a,\eta J\bar{f}}*Q^{\prime*})_{\nu^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\Gamma_{a}*J\bar{f})^{C}*Q^{\prime*})_{\nu^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\bar{\alpha}^{C}*Q^{\prime*})_{\nu^{\prime}}}}

where again we let
(\bar{f},\bar{\alpha},(f,\theta,\varphi))\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}(f,\alpha,\varphi)
as in
4.2
.

Moreover, note that for each object
(b,h\colon Jb\to\widetilde{T}C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}^{2}C
, the family of arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}Q(x)=a^{C}Tc_{x}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(aJc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})=a_{x}^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{a,\eta Jc_{x}})_{\nu_{x}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Gamma_{a}*Jc_{x})^{C}_{\nu_{x}}}}

for
x\in Jb
defines a natural transformation
\tau\colon a^{C}\circ Q\Rightarrow\Phi\circ h
, where as usual we denote
h(x)
as
(Rx,a_{x}\colon JRx\to C,\nu_{x})
and hence
\Phi(h(x))=a_{x}^{C}(\nu_{x})
. Therefore, we define the component of
\mathfrak{m}
at
(b,h,\nu)
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,h,\nu)=a^{C}Q^{*}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}Q)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi h)^{C}(\nu)=(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{a,Q})_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau^{C}_{\nu}}}

in
C
. We omit the routine proof of the naturality of
\mathfrak{m}
. The proof of the fact that
\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}
is a colax algebra for
\widetilde{T}
is postponed to Lemma
B.1
.

5.1.2.
On morphisms

Let
\braket{U,\upsilon}\colon\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}\to\braket{C^{\prime},(-)^{C^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}}
be a colax morphism of colax algebras for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
. The same underlying functor
U\colon C\to C^{\prime}
yields a colax morphism

\Theta(\braket{U,\upsilon})\coloneqq\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\colon\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}\to\braket{C^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\mathfrak{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}

of colax algebras for
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
by taking the natural transformation
\mathfrak{u}\colon U\circ\Phi\Rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{T}U
defined at an object
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}C
by the arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(U\circ\Phi)(b,h,\nu)=U(h^{C}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Uh)^{C}(\nu)=(\Phi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{T}U)(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\upsilon_{h})_{\nu}}}

in
C^{\prime}
. Naturality of
\mathfrak{u}
follows immediately by that of the family
\set{\upsilon}
. A proof of the fact that
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}
is a colax morphism is given in Lemma
B.2
. With this definition,
\Theta
is clearly a functor
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})
.

5.1.3.
On transformations

Let
\braket{U,\upsilon},\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}\colon\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}\to\braket{C^{\prime},(-)^{C^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta}
be colax morphisms of colax algebras for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
and let
\alpha\colon\braket{U,\upsilon}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}
be a transformation. Let us show that
\alpha
is also a transformation
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
, i.e. that the diagram of natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\circ\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}\circ\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{T}U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{T}U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha*\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}*\widetilde{T}\alpha}

commutes. Instantiated at an object
(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}C
, this yields the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Uh^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}h^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Uh)^{C^{\prime}}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(U^{\prime}h)^{C^{\prime}}(\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha*h^{C})_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\upsilon_{h})_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\upsilon^{\prime}_{h})_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha*h)^{C^{\prime}}_{\nu}}

in
C^{\prime}
, which commutes since
\alpha
is a transformation
\braket{U,\upsilon}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we can extend
\Theta
to a 2-functor
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})
simply by setting:

\Theta(\alpha)\coloneqq\alpha\colon\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}

Remark 5.5
.

It is evident how to define
\Theta
on lax morphisms and their transformations, and moreover that it preserves pseudomorphisms and strict morphisms. Therefore, we have defined a 2-functor
\Theta\colon{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}^{J}(T)\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}(\widetilde{T})
for each
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}
.

5.2.
From
\widetilde{T}
-algebras to
T
-algebras

We now build a 2-functor
\Sigma\colon{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)
. Although we will not make use of this intuition,
\Sigma
can be thought of as being induced by
\zeta\colon T\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}\circ S
acting as a ‘morphism of relative pseudomonads’ where we allow the root 2-functor to change – a notion which does not yet appear in the literature.

5.2.1.
On algebras

Let
\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}
be a colax algebra for the pseudomonad
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
. We define a colax algebra

\Sigma(\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}})\coloneqq\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}

for the
J
-relative 2-monad
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
as follows.

We define the extension operator
(-)^{C}\colon\left[J-,C\right]\Rightarrow\left[T-,C\right]
as the unique lax transformation corresponding to the functor
\Phi\colon\widetilde{T}C\to C
, that is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[J-,C\right]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[T-,\widetilde{T}C\right]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left[T-,C\right].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{T,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi\circ-}

Next we define a natural transformation
\Gamma_{h}\colon h^{C}\circ\eta_{b}\Rightarrow h
for each functor
h\colon Jb\to C
. First note that
h^{C}\circ\eta_{b}\colon Jb\to C
is the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\eta_{b}(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\eta_{b}(x^{\prime}))\ .}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id}_{b},\operatorname{id},\eta_{b}(g))}

Therefore, we define
\Gamma_{h}
on an object
x\in Jb
– identified with a functor
1_{{\bf CAT}}\to Jb
– as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\eta_{b}(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(1_{\mathcal{B}},hx\colon 1_{{\bf CAT}}\to C,\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\dot{x},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{h(x)}}

in
C
, where
\dot{x}\colon 1_{\mathcal{B}}\to b
is the unique 1-cell in
\mathcal{B}
such that
J\dot{x}=x
, by assumption of 2-fully-faithfulness of
J
. We omit the routine proofs of the naturality of each
\Gamma_{h}
and of the family
\set{\Gamma}
.

Finally, we define a natural transformation
\Delta_{h,k}\colon h^{C}\circ k^{*}\Rightarrow(h^{C}\circ k)^{C}
for each pair of functors
h\colon Jb\to C
and
k\colon Jc\to Tb
. First note that
h^{C}\circ k^{*}
and
(h^{C}\circ k)^{C}
are the functors
Tc\to C
respectively given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,h\colon Jb\to C,k^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nu^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,h\colon Jb\to C,k^{*}(\nu^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id},k^{*}(\varphi))}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(c,h^{C}k\colon Jc\to C,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nu^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(c,h^{C}k\colon Jc\to C,\nu^{\prime})\ .}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id},\varphi)}

Therefore, we define
\Delta_{h,k}
on an object
\nu\in Tc
as the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,h\colon Jb\to C,k^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(c,\iota_{b}(h)k\colon Jc\to\widetilde{T}C,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(c,\iota_{b}(h)k\colon Jc\to\widetilde{T}C,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=\Phi(c,h^{C}k\colon Jc\to C,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(t,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(c,\iota_{b}(h)k,\nu)}}

in
C
, where we let
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(c,Q\colon Jc\to T\ell,\nu))\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}(c,\iota_{b}(h)k,\nu)
as in
4.2
and use
t\colon\ell\to b\in\mathcal{B}
to denote the canonical 1-cell induced being
b
an oplax cocone on the constant diagram
{(Jc)}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{B}
of value
b
, of which
\ell
is the oplax colimit. We omit the routine proofs of the naturality of each
\Delta_{h,k}
and of the family
\set{\Delta}
. The proof of the fact that
\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}
is a colax algebra for
T
is postponed to Lemma
B.3
.

5.2.2.
On morphisms

Let
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\colon\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}\to\braket{C^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\mathfrak{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
be a colax morphism of colax algebras for
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
. The same underlying functor
U\colon C\to C^{\prime}
yields a colax morphism

\Sigma(\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}})\coloneqq\braket{U,\upsilon}\colon\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}\to\braket{C^{\prime},(-)^{C^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}}

of colax algebras for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
by taking, for a functor
h\colon Jb\to C
, the natural transformation
\upsilon_{h}\colon Uh^{C}\Rightarrow(Uh)^{C^{\prime}}
defined at an object
\nu\in Tb
by the arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Uh^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=(U\circ\Phi)(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}U)(b,h\colon Jb\to C,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=\Phi(b,Uh\colon Jb\to C^{\prime},\nu)=(Uh)^{C^{\prime}}(\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{u}_{(b,h,\nu)}}}

in
C^{\prime}
. Naturality of
\upsilon_{h}
and of the family
\set{\upsilon}
follow immediately by that of
\mathfrak{u}
. A proof of the fact that
\braket{U,\upsilon}
is a colax morphism is given in Lemma
B.4
. With this definition,
\Sigma
is clearly a functor
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)
.

5.2.3.
On transformations

Let
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}},\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}\colon\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}\to\braket{C^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\mathfrak{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
be colax morphisms of colax algebras for
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
and let
\alpha\colon\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
be a transformation. Let us show that
\alpha
is also a transformation
\braket{U,\upsilon}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}
, i.e. that the diagram of natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Uh^{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}h^{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Uh)^{C^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(U^{\prime}h)^{C^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha*h^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon^{\prime}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha*h)^{C}}

commutes for each functor
h\colon Jb\to C
. Instantiated at an object
\nu\in Tb
, this yields the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(U\circ\Phi)(b,h,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(U^{\prime}\circ\Phi)(b,h,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}U)(b,h,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}U^{\prime})(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha*\Phi)_{(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{u}_{(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}_{(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Phi*\widetilde{T}\alpha)_{(b,h,\nu)}}

which commutes since
\alpha
is a transformation
\braket{U,\mathfrak{u}}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
. Therefore, we can extend
\Sigma
to a 2-functor
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}(\widetilde{T})\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{co}^{J}(T)
simply by setting:

\Sigma(\alpha)\coloneqq\alpha\colon\braket{U,\upsilon}\Rightarrow\braket{U^{\prime},\upsilon^{\prime}}

Remark 5.6
.

As for
\Theta
, it is evident how to define
\Sigma
on lax morphisms and their transformations, and moreover that it preserves pseudomorphisms and strict morphisms. Therefore, we have defined a 2-functor
\Sigma\colon{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}(\widetilde{T})\to{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}^{J}(T)
for each
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}

5.3.
There and back again

Finally, we complete the proof of Theorem
5.4
by showing that
\Theta
and
\Sigma
are each other’s inverse. First, consider the composite
\Sigma\circ\Theta
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}
be a colax algebra for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
. Then

\Sigma\Theta(\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta})=(\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}).

Proof.

Denote
\Sigma\Theta(\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta})
as
\braket{C,(-)^{\bar{C}},\bar{\Gamma},\bar{\Delta}}
. Clearly
(-)^{\bar{C}}=(-)^{C}
as they are both the unique lax transformation corresponding to the functor
\Phi\colon\widetilde{T}C\to C
defining
\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}\coloneqq\Theta(\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta})
.

For a functor
h\colon Jb\to C
, the natural transformation
\bar{\Gamma}_{h}
is defined at an object
x\in Jb
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,h,\eta_{b}(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(1_{\mathcal{B}},hx,\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\dot{x},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{i}_{h(x)}}}

which, using the description of
\Phi
and
\mathfrak{i}
, is explicitly the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(\eta_{b}(x))=h^{C}T\dot{x}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}x)^{C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hx)^{C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(x).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}x})_{\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{h}*x)^{C}_{\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Gamma_{hx})_{\bullet}}}}}

Note then that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(\eta_{b}(x))=h^{C}T\dot{x}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}x)^{C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hx)^{C}(\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}}(\bullet))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}x)(\bullet)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hx(\bullet)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}x}*\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}})_{\bullet}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{((\Gamma_{h}*x)^{C}*\eta_{1_{\mathcal{B}}})_{\bullet}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Gamma_{h^{C}\eta_{b}x})_{\bullet}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Gamma_{hx})_{\bullet}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Gamma_{h}*x)_{\bullet}}}

commutes by naturality of the family
\set{\Gamma}
with respect to
\Gamma_{h}*x
and by axiom (b) of a colax algebra (see Definition
1.4
). Therefore,
(\bar{\Gamma}_{h})_{x}=(\Gamma_{h})_{x}
, which entails that
\bar{\Gamma}=\Gamma
.

For a pair of functors
h\colon Jb\to C,k\colon Jc\to Tb
, the natural transformation
\bar{\Delta}_{h,k}
is defined at an object
\nu\in Tc
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,h,k^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\ell,a,Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(c,\iota_{b}(h)k,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(c,\iota_{b}(h)k,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=\Phi(c,h^{C}k,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Phi(t,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathfrak{m}_{(c,\iota_{b}(h)k,\nu)}}}}

where
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(c,Q\colon Jc\to T\ell,\nu))\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}(c,\iota_{b}(h)k,\nu)
as in
5.2.1
and where
t\colon\ell\to b\in\mathcal{B}
is the canonical 1-cell induced being
b
an oplax cocone on the diagram of which
\ell
is the oplax colimit. Using the explicit description of
\Phi
and
\mathfrak{m}
, we can rewrite the previous as the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(k^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}Tt(Q^{*}\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt)^{C}(Q^{*}\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hJt)^{C}(Q^{*}\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}(Q^{*}\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}Q)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}k)^{C}(\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jt})_{Q^{*}\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{h}*Jt)^{C}_{Q^{*}\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\Delta_{a,Q})_{\nu}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\tau^{C}_{\nu}}}}

Consider now the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}Tt(Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(k^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt)^{C}(Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt)^{C}Q)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hJt)^{C}(Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}(Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((hJt)^{C}Q)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}TtQ)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}Q)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}k)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}k)^{C}(\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jt}*Q^{*})_{\nu}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{h,k})_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt,Q})_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{((\Gamma_{h}*Jt)^{C}*Q^{*})_{\nu}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((\Gamma_{h}*Jt)^{C}*Q)^{C}_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{hJt,Q})_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\Delta_{a,Q})_{\nu}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jt}*Q)^{C}_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\tau^{C}_{\nu}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(ii)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(iii)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(iv)}}

where (i) commutes by axiom (c) of a colax algebra, (ii) commutes by naturality of the family
\set{\Delta}
with respect to
\Gamma_{h}*Jt
, and (iii) commutes since
h\circ Jt=a
. To see why (iv) also commutes, note that by functoriality of
(-)^{C}
it suffices to show that the diagram of natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hJt)^{C}Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt)^{C}Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}TtQ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{h}*Jt)^{C}*Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jt}*Q}

commutes. Instantiated at
x\in Jc
, this yields the outer border of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hJt)^{C}Tc_{x}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt)^{C}(Tc_{x}(\nu_{x}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{C}Tc_{x}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt)^{C}\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}\eta_{b}JtJc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{C}Tt\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(aJc_{x})^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}(\nu_{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{C}TtTc_{x}(\nu_{x})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\Gamma_{h}*Jt)^{C}*Tc_{x})_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt,\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x}})_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{a,\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x}})_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\Gamma_{h}*Jt)^{C}*\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x})^{C}_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{h^{C}\eta_{b}Jt}*Jc_{x})^{C}_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{a}*Jc_{x})^{C}_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Gamma_{h}*JtJc_{x})^{C}_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jt}*\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x})^{C}_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h,\eta_{b}Jt}*Tc_{x})_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h,Tt\eta_{\ell}Jc_{x}})_{\nu_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(iv^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(ii^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(iii^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v^{\prime})}

in
C
, which commutes since:

(i’)

commutes by naturality of the family
\set{\Delta}
with respect to
\Gamma_{h}*Jt
, using the fact that
h\circ Jt=a
;

(ii’)

commutes by naturality of the family
\set{\Gamma}
with respect to
\Gamma_{h}*Jt
, again using the fact that
h\circ Jt=a
;

(iii’)

commutes by axiom (b) of a colax algebra;

(iv’)

commutes by axiom (c) of a colax algebra;

(v’)

commutes by axiom (a) of a colax algebra, using the fact that
Tt\circ\eta_{\ell}=\eta_{b}\circ Jt
by naturality of
\eta
, that
t\circ c_{x}=\operatorname{id}_{b}
, and that
h\circ Jt=a
.

Summing up, we have shown that
(\bar{\Delta}_{h,k})_{\nu}
coincides with
(\Delta_{h,k})_{\nu}
, which entails that
\bar{\Delta}=\Delta
, concluding the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

The composite
\Sigma\circ\Theta
is the identity 2-functor on
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}^{J}(T)
.

Proof.

It is immediate to see that
\Sigma\circ\Theta
acts as the identity on morphisms of colax algebras for
\braket{T,\eta,(-)^{*}}
. As both
\Theta
and
\Sigma
act as the identity on transformations, we conclude that
\Sigma\circ\Theta=\operatorname{id}
as 2-functors on
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}^{J}(T)
.
∎

On the other hand, consider now the composite
\Theta\circ\Sigma
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}
be a colax algebra for
\braket{\widetilde{T},\eta^{\sharp},\mu^{\sharp}}
. Then

\Theta\Sigma\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}=\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}.

Proof.

Denote
\Theta\Sigma\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}
as
\braket{C,\bar{\Phi},\bar{\mathfrak{i}},\bar{\mathfrak{m}}}
. Clearly
\bar{\Phi}=\Phi
as they are both the unique functor corresponding to the lax transformation
(-)^{C}\colon\left[J-,C\right]\Rightarrow\left[T-,C\right]
defining
\braket{C,(-)^{C},\Gamma,\Delta}\coloneqq\Sigma(\braket{C,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}})
. Moreover, it is immediate to see that
\bar{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathfrak{i}
.

It remains to show that
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}
. The natural transformation
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}
is defined at an object
(b,h\colon Jb\to\widetilde{T}C,\nu)\in\widetilde{T}^{2}C
as the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,h,\nu)=a^{C}Q^{*}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a^{C}Q)^{C}(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi h)^{C}(\nu)=(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\Delta_{a,Q})_{\nu}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\tau^{C}_{\nu}}}}

where we let
(\ell,a\colon J\ell\to C,(b,Q\colon Jb\to T\ell,\nu))\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}(b,h,\nu)
as in
5.1.1
, and where
\tau\colon a^{C}Q\Rightarrow\Phi\circ h
is the natural transformation defined also in
5.1.1
. Unwrapping the definition of
(-)^{C}
, the previous composite is equal to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi\iota_{\ell}(a)(Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\iota_{b}(\Phi\iota_{\ell}(a)Q))(\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\iota_{b}(\Phi h))(\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{a,Q})_{\nu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Phi*\iota_{b}(\tau))_{\nu}}

and hence, unwrapping the definition of
\Delta_{a,Q}
, also equal to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\ell,a,Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\bar{\ell},\bar{a},\bar{Q}^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=\Phi(b,a^{C}Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,\Phi h,\nu),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(t,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\operatorname{id},\tau,\operatorname{id})}

where we let
(\bar{\ell},\bar{a}\colon J\bar{\ell}\to C,(b,\bar{Q}\colon Jb\to T\bar{\ell},\nu))\coloneqq\mathfrak{s}_{T,T}(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)
and where
t\colon\bar{\ell}\to\ell\in\mathcal{B}
is the canonical 1-cell induced being
\ell
an oplax cocone on the diagram of which
\bar{\ell}
is the oplax colimit. Note then that
\tau=\Phi*\tau^{\prime}
where
\tau^{\prime}\colon\iota_{\ell}(a)Q\Rightarrow h
is the natural transformation defined at an object
x\in Jb
by the arrow

(c_{x},\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})\colon\iota_{\ell}(a)(Q(x))=(\ell,a,Tc_{x}(\nu_{x}))\to(Rx,a_{x},\nu_{x})=h(x)

in
\widetilde{T}C
. Therefore,
\Phi(\operatorname{id},\tau,\operatorname{id})
coincides with
(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(\operatorname{id},\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{id})
by definition of
\widetilde{T}\Phi
, and hence by naturality of
\mathfrak{m}
we have that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,h,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(b,h,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\widetilde{T}\Phi)(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=\Phi(b,a^{C}Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(b,\Phi h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(\operatorname{id},\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}_{(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\operatorname{id},\tau,\operatorname{id})}

commutes. Moreover, by the universal properties of oplax colimits (and by functoriality of
\Phi
), the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\ell,a,Q^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\bar{\ell},\bar{a},\bar{Q}^{*}(\nu))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,\iota_{\ell}(a)Q,\nu)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(b,h,\nu)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(t,\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Phi\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{C})(\operatorname{id},\tau^{\prime},\operatorname{id})}

also commutes. Summing up, we have shown that
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{(b,h,\nu)}
coincides with
\mathfrak{m}_{(b,h,\nu)}
, which means that
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}
, concluding the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

The composite
\Theta\circ\Sigma
is the identity 2-functor on
{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}(\widetilde{T})
.

Proof.

Analogous to Corollary
5.8
.
∎

6.
The ultracompletion

We now arrive at the intended application of Theorem
5.4
. In the case of the relative ultrafilter 2-monad
\braket{J\beta,J\eta,(-)^{\ast}}
of Section
2
, the hypotheses of Section
4
are satisfied. In particular, since
{\bf Set}
is discrete on 2-cells, (small) oplax colimits are simply conical colimits, which
{\bf Set}
admits and the inclusion
J\colon{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
preserves (cf. Remark
4.1
). Therefore, we can deduce that weak ultracategories are the colax algebras for a universally obtained pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
, for which Rosolini suggested the name
ultracompletion
in
[
Ros24
]
. Note that, in this section, by Remark
3.12
we can replace
{\bf CAT}
with the 2-category of locally small categories.

Corollary 6.1
.

Weak ultracategories are the colax algebras for a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
whose underlying 2-endofunctor
\widetilde{\beta}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
is (up to isomorphism) the unique 2-functor such that
(\beta,\rho)
is a right Kan extension and
(\widetilde{\beta},\zeta)
is a left oplax Kan extension in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf FinSet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf CAT}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf CAT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Proof.

By Proposition
3.10
we know that
(\widetilde{\beta},\zeta)
is the left oplax Kan extension of the composite
\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
along the inclusion
{\bf Set}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
. By the above, we can then apply Theorem
5.4
to the relative ultrafilter 2-monad, obtaining that
\mathbf{WUlt}_{\star}\cong{\rm ColaxAlg}_{\star}(\widetilde{\beta})
for each
\star\in\set{co,lax,ps,str}
. Finally, we combine this with the well-known fact that the ultrafilter monad
\beta\colon{\bf Set}\to{\bf Set}
is the codensity monad of the inclusion
{\bf FinSet}\hookrightarrow{\bf Set}
(
[
KG71
,
Man76
,
Lei13
]
).
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Our ‘ultracompletion’ pseudomonad
\widetilde{\beta}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
strongly resembles the pseudomonad constructed in
[
Ham25
]
– and hence also that of
[
Ros24
]
– but is not identical. This is because our weak ultracategories, i.e. the colax algebras for
\widetilde{\beta}
, satisfy a weaker axiomatisation than Lurie’s ultracategories (Definition
2.4
). The latter, in turn, are the
normal
colax algebras for Hamad’s pseudomonad, where a colax algebra
\braket{M,\Phi,\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{m}}
is normal if the counitor
\mathfrak{i}
is a natural isomorphism.

6.1.
Prime categories

One benefit of our general treatment involving arbitrary relative 2-monads acting on
{\bf CAT}
is that we can apply the same results to analogues of ultracategories. To conclude, we discuss a generalisation of ultracategories where the role of ultrafilters and ultraproducts is played by
prime filters
and the
prime product
construction from positive model theory
[
MWY23
]
. Our hope is that this may lead to an ordered generalisation of the “Stone duality for first-order logic” of
[
Mak87
]
, as a kind of
Priestley
duality for first-order logic.

As we have seen, ultracategories can be interpreted as a categorical analogue of compact Hausdorff spaces, which are precisely the
ultrasets
, i.e. those ultracategories whose carrier category is a set (Remark
2.7
). In particular, among these are Stone spaces, which are the spaces of models of classical propositional theories by Stone duality for Boolean algebras. In the ordered setting, compact Hausdorff spaces are replaced by
compact ordered spaces
; among these, Priestley spaces correspond to spaces of models of coherent propositional theories through Priestley duality. In
[
Fla97
]
, the category of compact ordered spaces and continuous order-preserving maps is proved to be monadic over the category
\mathbf{Pos}
of posets, similarly to Manes’ theorem
[
Man69
]
for compact Hausdorff spaces.

The monad on
\mathbf{Pos}
witnessing the monadicity result is the
prime upper filter monad

\braket{B,\eta,(-)^{*}}
, and it maps a poset
P
to the poset
BP\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}(\operatorname{Up}(P))
of prime filters of the lattice
\operatorname{Up}(P)
of upward-closed subsets of
P
. Moreover,
B
is a 2-monad on
\mathbf{Pos}
seen as a locally-thin 2-category via the pointwise order on its homsets. In particular, the results of
[
AS21
]
show that
\braket{B,\eta,(-)^{*}}
is the codensity 2-monad of the inclusion
\mathbf{FinPos}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pos}
, meaning that
B
is the right Kan extension – in the 2-categorical sense of
[
Kel05
, §4.1]
– of
\mathbf{FinPos}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pos}
along itself. Therefore, as the inclusion
J\colon\mathbf{Pos}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
of posets into categories is clearly 2-fully faithful, by Example
1.3
we obtain a relative 2-monad
\braket{JB,J\eta,(-)^{*}}
over
J\colon\mathbf{Pos}\to{\bf CAT}
, which we call the
relative prime upper filter 2-monad
. This allows us to give the following definition.

Definition 6.3
.

A
prime category
is a colax algebra for the relative prime upper filter 2-monad.

Example 6.4
.

Analogously to the case of ultracategories, the category
\mathbf{Pos}
carries the archetypal structure of a prime category. A monotone function
h\colon P\to\mathbf{Pos}
can be identified with an
ordered system
in the sense of
[
MWY23
]
, i.e. a sequence of posets
\set{M_{p}}_{p\in P}
together with a family of monotone maps
\set{f_{p,p^{\prime}}\colon M_{p}\to M_{p^{\prime}}}_{p\leqslant p^{\prime}}
satisfying:

(1)

f_{p,p}=\operatorname{id}_{M_{p}}
for each
p\in P
;

(2)

f_{p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}}\circ f_{p,p^{\prime}}=f_{p,p^{\prime\prime}}
if
p\leqslant p^{\prime}\leqslant p^{\prime\prime}
.

The extension
h^{\mathbf{Pos}}\colon BP\to\mathbf{Pos}
acts by sending a prime upper filter
\nu\in BP
to the
prime product
of the system
h
with respect to
\nu
, as constructed in
[
MWY23
, Definition 2.4]
:

h^{\mathbf{Pos}}(\nu)\coloneqq\colim_{U\in{\nu}^{\operatorname{op}}}\left(\lim_{p\in U}M_{p}\right)

Example 6.5
.

By
[
Fla97
, Theorem 12]
, the
strict
prime categories whose carrier category is a poset correspond to compact ordered spaces (cf. Example
1.8
and Remark
2.7
).

Note that the inclusion
J\colon\mathbf{Pos}\hookrightarrow{\bf CAT}
satisfies the hypotheses of Section
4
. To see why
J
preserves oplax colimits of diagrams indexed by a poset, let
F\colon I\to\mathbf{Pos}
be such a diagram. Then
J\circ F\colon I\to{\bf CAT}
admits an oplax colimit
L
in
{\bf CAT}
, given by its Grothendieck construction; explicitly, this means that
L
is the category having:

(1)

as objects, pairs
(i,x)
of an element
i\in I
and an element
x\in F(i)
;

(2)

an arrow
(i,x)\leqslant(i^{\prime},x^{\prime})
if and only if
i\leqslant i^{\prime}
in
I
and
F(i\leqslant i^{\prime})(x)\leqslant x^{\prime}
in
F(x^{\prime})
.

In particular,
L
itself is a poset. As
J
is 2-fully faithful, it reflects all 2-colimits (among which are oplax colimits), meaning that
L
is the oplax colimit of the diagram
F
in
\mathbf{Pos}
. This yields the following.

Corollary 6.6
.

Prime categories are the colax algebras for a pseudomonad on
{\bf CAT}
whose underlying 2-endofunctor
\widetilde{B}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
is (up to isomorphism) the unique 2-functor such that
(B,\rho)
is a right Kan extension and
(\widetilde{B},\zeta)
is a left oplax Kan extension in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{FinPos}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf CAT}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf CAT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

Proof.

Analogous to Corollary
6.1
.
∎

Appendix A
Oplax coends

As we show in Section
3
, left oplax Kan extension in
{\bf CAT}
can be calculated by means of
oplax coends
. In this appendix, we recall the definitions of (op)lax (co)ends and we prove some of their properties relating them to left oplax Kan extensions.

Definition A.1
.

Fix a 2-functor
H\colon{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
. An
oplax cowedge
on
H
, denoted by
\gamma\colon H\to A
, consists of:

(1)

an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, called the
vertex
;

(2)

for each object
b\in\mathcal{B}
, a 1-cell
\gamma_{b}\colon H(b,b)\to A
in
\mathcal{A}
;

(3)

for each 1-cell
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b\in\mathcal{B}
, a 2-cell
\gamma_{f}\colon\gamma_{b}\circ H(\operatorname{id},f)\Rightarrow\gamma_{b^{\prime}}\circ H(f,\operatorname{id})
,

satisfying the following axioms:

(a)

for each object
b\in\mathcal{B}
, the 2-cell
\gamma_{\operatorname{id}_{b}}
is the identity on
\gamma_{b}
;

(b)

for each composable pair of 1-cells
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b
and
g\colon b^{\prime\prime}\to b^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}
, the pasting on the left coincides with that on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A,}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(g,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b^{\prime\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},gf)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(gf,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(g,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b^{\prime\prime}}}

(c)

for each parallel pair of 1-cells
f,g\colon b^{\prime}\to b
and each 2-cell
\sigma\colon f\Rightarrow g
in
\mathcal{B}
, the pasting on the left coincides with that on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b),}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(g,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{g}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b).}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(g,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\sigma,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{f}}

Given two oplax cowedges
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\colon H\to A
, a
modification

\Psi\colon\gamma\Rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}
is a family of 2-cells
\Psi_{b}\colon\gamma_{b}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}_{b}
such that, for each 1-cell
f\colon b^{\prime}\to b\in\mathcal{B}
, the pasting on the left coincides with that on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b),}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{b^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b).}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f,\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\operatorname{id},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{b}}

A
lax cowedge
on
H
is an oplax cowedge on the 2-functor
H^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}\times{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{A}
defined by
(b,b^{\prime})\mapsto H(b^{\prime},b)
. Dually, an
(op)lax wedge
on
H
is an (op)lax cowedge in
{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Transformations thereof are defined in the obvious way.

Notation A.2
.

For an oplax cowedge
\gamma\colon H\to A
and a 1-cell
u\colon A\to A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, we denote with
u\gamma
the oplax cowedge
H\to A^{\prime}
defined by the families
\set{u\circ\gamma_{b}}
and
\set{u*\gamma_{f}}
.

Definition A.3
.

The
oplax coend
of
H
consists of an object
\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.78818pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 8.78818pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.49306pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 8.49306pt}}H(b,b)\in\mathcal{A}
together with an oplax cowedge
\lambda\colon H\to\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.78818pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 8.78818pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.49306pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 8.49306pt}}H(b,b)
with the folllowing universal properties.

(1)

For each oplax cowedge
\gamma\colon H\to A
, there exists a unique 1-cell
u\colon\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.78818pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 8.78818pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.49306pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 8.49306pt}}H(b,b)\to A
in
\mathcal{B}
such that
\gamma=u\lambda
, i.e. that for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.78818pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 8.78818pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.49306pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 8.49306pt}}H(b,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(b,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{b}}

commutes.

(2)

For each pair of 1-cells
u,v\colon\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-9.84998pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 9.84998pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.78818pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 8.78818pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int^{b}$}\hskip-8.49306pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 8.49306pt}}H(b,b)\to A
and each modification
\Psi\colon u\lambda\Rightarrow v\lambda
, there exists a unique 2-cell
\psi\colon u\Rightarrow v
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\Psi=\psi*\lambda
, i.e. that
\Psi_{b}=\psi*\lambda_{b}
for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

If it exists, an oplax coend is clearly unique up to isomorphism. Similar universal properties define
lax coends
and
(op)lax ends
.

Fix a pair of 2-functors
J,T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
. Recall by Definition
3.3
that a 2-functor
\widetilde{T}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is the
left oplax Kan extension
of
T
along
J
if, for any pair
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
, there is an isomorphism of categories

{\mathcal{A}}({\widetilde{T}a},{a^{\prime}})\cong\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-18.18416pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 18.18416pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-17.88904pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 17.88904pt}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{a}),{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{a^{\prime}})\right].

In these terms, we can easily derive the following.

Proposition A.4
.

If
\widetilde{T}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is the left oplax Kan extension of
T\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
along
J\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
, then for each 2-functor
S\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
there is an isomorphism of categories:

\mathbf{Str}\left[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\right](\widetilde{T},S)\cong\mathbf{Oplax}\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}\right]\left(T,S\circ J\right)

which is natural in
S
.

Proof.

The proof is formally identical to that of
[
Kel05
, Theorem 4.38]
, up to considering oplax ends instead of ends. Indeed, we have the following chain of isomorphisms, all natural in
S
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Str}\left[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\right](\widetilde{T},S)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{a\in\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}({\widetilde{T}a},{Sa})

(
[
Kel05
, §2.10]
)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-18.18416pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 18.18416pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-17.88904pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 17.88904pt}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{a}),{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{Sa})\right]

\displaystyle\cong\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-18.18416pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 18.18416pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-17.88904pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 17.88904pt}}\int_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left[{\mathcal{A}}({Jb},{a}),{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{Sa})\right]

(
[
Hir22
, §3.6]
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathchoice{\hbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-20.82918pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\hskip-0.89996pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.87776pt\hskip 20.82918pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-18.18416pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hskip-0.73747pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-6.13747pt\hskip 18.18416pt}}{\hbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int_{b\in\mathcal{B}}$}\hskip-17.88904pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-0.66248pt\rightarrow$}}\hskip-5.79582pt\hskip 17.88904pt}}{\mathcal{A}}({Tb},{SJb})

(
[
Kel05
, §2.33]
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Oplax}\left[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}\right]\left(T,S\circ J\right).

(
[
Hir22
, §3.1]
)

In particular, by naturality in
S
, the unique oplax transformation
\zeta\colon T\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}\circ J
corresponding to the strict transformation
\operatorname{id}\colon\widetilde{T}\Rightarrow\widetilde{T}
exhibits
\widetilde{T}
as the weak left oplax Kan extension of
T
along
J
.
∎

Let us now restrict to
{\bf CAT}
-valued 2-functors.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
J,T\colon\mathcal{B}\to{\bf CAT}
be 2-functors. Up to isomorphism, the left oplax Kan extension
\widetilde{T}\colon{\bf CAT}\to{\bf CAT}
of
T
along
J
is defined by setting, for each category
C
:
